TITLE:   Designing an AIOps (Artificial Intelligent Operations) Framework for Energy-Efficient IT Operations: A Case Study on Cloud Computing Environment
AUTHORS: ['Milankumar Rana', 'Sakhita Sree Gadde']
YEAR:    2025   VENUE: 2025 IEEE International Symposium on Future Telecommunication Technologies (SOFTT)

TAGS:    []
-- ABSTRACT --
The paper proposes a six-layer AIOps framework that integrates energy metrics with traditional IT operations data to enable machine-learning-driven, automated energy optimizations in cloud environments. A six-month case study in a mid-sized cloud deployment demonstrated about 27% total energy reduction (with 32% cooling savings), improved server utilization, maintained or improved application performance, and a positive ROI with a short payback period.
-- METHOD --
The paper proposes and implements a six-layer AIOps framework focused on energy-efficient IT operations in a cloud environment. The architecture comprises: (1) Data collection and integration of infrastructure, energy, application performance, environmental and workload metrics via standardized protocols/APIs; (2) Data processing and storage with preprocessing (missing-value handling, outlier removal, time-series alignment) and retention policies in a time-series-optimized store; (3) Analytics and machine learning including descriptive, diagnostic, predictive and prescriptive analytics using regression, classification, clustering and reinforcement-learning models; (4) Knowledge management st

Designing an AIOps (Artificial Intelligent Operations) 
Framework for Energy-Efficient IT Operations: A

Abstract- This study looks at using AI and quantum computing 
to increase the security and efficiency of financial transactions. 
The suggested system's hybrid architecture enables autonomous 
agents to work together to make decisions. Computer performance 
is enhanced and accelerated by quantum techniques. Grover's 
algorithm speeds up the process of finding the best solution and 
confirming transactions. Quantum key distribution (QKD) 
protocols are designed to be impenetrable and protect data. Agent-
based utility models are even more flexible and scalable because 
they can adapt to the needs of the operational environment. The 
design places a high value on adaptability, ease of installation 
across multiple platforms, and compatibility with other systems. It 
has a cloud-friendly design. Real-world testing demonstrates that 
the new techniques are far more effective than the previous ones. 
They reduced transaction throughput by 64%, vulnerability 
exposure by 89%, and processing time by 42%. Additionally, user 
experience research indicates that when they are not required to 
assist as much, people are 37% more likely to complete a 
transaction. The findings demonstrate that the application of agent-
driven systems to the development of business and e-commerce 
platforms can be altered by quantum technology. Future 
development of new digital transaction ecosystems will be made 
easier by the suggested solution, which concurrently addresses 
security, adaptability, and operational efficiency. 
Keyword- Aiops, energy efficiency, cloud computing, sustainable 
it, machine learning, predictive analytics, green computing, it 
operations, resource optimization 
I. INTRODUCTION 
Data centers all around the world have significantly increased 
their energy consumption while cloud computing's exponential 
expansion is occurring. Recent estimates place data centers' 
consumption at 1–2% of world electricity at 0.3% of total carbon 
emissions [1]. The environmental impact of these digital 
infrastructures grows more alarming as companies keep moving 
their activities to the cloud. Concurrent with this development in 
complexity of managing contemporary IT settings, intelligent 
automation and decisio
-- RESULTS WINDOW 2 [3141:6568] --
t mostly unrealized. 
This work presents a new AIOps architecture intended 
especially for energy-efficient IT operations in cloud computing 
systems. To give a whole picture of cloud infrastructure 
performance, the framework combines conventional operational 
data with energy usage measures. Applying machine learning 
techniques to this integrated information helps the framework to 
automatically apply optimization tactics, detect energy 
inefficiencies, and forecast consumption patterns. 
This paper's main contributions consist of An all-
encompassing AIOps framework architecture, including energy 
efficiency as a fundamental IT operation concern, technique for 
gathering, combining, and evaluating operational as well as 
energy-related data in cloud systems, collection of machine 
learning algorithms for estimating energy use and spotting areas 
of optimization. Empirical confirmation of the theory through a 
case study executed in a mid-sized cloud environment. 
Quantitative evaluation of performance effects and energy 
savings accomplished with the framework 
Data centers need a lot more energy because cloud computing 
is growing so quickly. To avoid performance risks, traditional IT 
operations often give out too many resources. Not only does this 
raise the cost of doing business, but it also wastes energy and 
hurts the environment. As sustainability becomes more 
important, there is a growing need for smart solutions that can 
manage and improve energy use without lowering the quality of 
service. 
Beyond operational efficiency, the proposed architecture also 
contributes to the broader sustainability goals of cloud service 
providers and enterprises. By integrating energy-awareness 
directly into AIOps pipelines, organizations can move from 
reactive energy monitoring to proactive optimization. This shift 
not only reduces unnecessary resource overprovisioning but also 
aligns IT operations with global carbon-reduction targets and 
corporate environmental, social, and governance (ESG) 
strategies. Furthermore, embedding energy considerations into 
automated decision-making processes ensures that sustainability 
does not come at the expense of service-level agreements (SLAs),

The remainder of this paper is organized as follows: Section 
II reviews related work in AIOps and energy-efficient cloud 
computing. Section III describes the methodology, including the 
framework architecture and implementation approach. Section 
IV presents the empirical results from our case study. Finally, 
Section V concludes the paper and discusses future research 
directions. 
II. RELATED WORK 
A. AIOps: Evolution and Current State 
Gartner originally used the term "AIOps" in 2017 to 
characterize how artificial intelligence, especially machine 
learning and data analytics particularly is used in IT operations 
[3]. Since then, AIOps has developed from a conceptual 
framework to a pragmati
-- RESULTS WINDOW 3 [9636:12336] --
on of these 
frameworks is still lacking. Furthermore, rather than including 
energy efficiency into a single management framework, most 
current methods view it as a separate issue from operational 
performance [17]. Our work expands on these bases by 
suggesting a thorough AIOps architecture based on energy 
efficiency as the focus of IT operations. From data collecting to 
automatic repair, we want to offer a complete solution to 
sustainable cloud computing by including energy concerns into 
every layer of the system. 
III. METHODOLOGY 
A. Framework Architecture 
The proposed AIOps framework for energy-efficient IT 
operations consists of six interconnected layers, each serving a 
specific function while contributing to the overall goal of 
optimizing energy efficiency. Fig. 1 illustrates the framework 
architecture. 
1) Layer 1: Data Collection and Integration 
 This layer is responsible for gathering data from various 
sources across the cloud infrastructure, including: Infrastructure 
metrics (CPU, memory, storage, network utilization), Energy 
consumption metrics (power draw, temperature, cooling 
efficiency), Application performance metrics (response time, 
throughput, 
error 
rates), 
Environmental 
data 
(ambient 
temperature, humidity, time of day), Workload characteristics 
(type, priority, resource requirements). 
The data collection components employ standardized 
protocols and APIs to interface with different monitoring 
systems, 
ensuring 
compatibility 
with 
diverse 
cloud 
environments. A data integration engine normalizes and 
correlates data from these disparate sources, creating a unified 
dataset for subsequent analysis. 
2) Layer 2: Data Processing and Storage 
The collected data undergoes preprocessing to handle missing 
values, remove outliers, and standardize formats. Time-series 
alignment ensures that data points from different sources can be 
correlated accurately. The processed data is stored in a scalable 
database optimized for time-series analytics, with appropriate 
retention policies based on data type and value. 
3) Layer 3: Analytics and Machine Learning 
This layer applies various analytical techniques to the 
integrated dataset, including Descriptive analytics to understand 
current energy consumption patterns, Diagnostic analytics to 
identify factors contributing to energy inefficiency, Predictive

A. Energy Consumption Reduction 
 The implementation of the AIOps framework resulted in 
significant energy consumption reduction across the cloud 
environment. Table I summarizes the energy consumption 
before and after implementation. 
 The most significant reductions were observed in cooling 
energy consumption (32%) and carbon usage effectiveness 
(29.3%), indicating that the framework was particularly 
effective at optimizing cooling operations and shifting 
workloads to periods of lower carbon intensity.

opportunities for workload optimization, leading to improved 
resource utilization and energy efficiency. Figure II illustrates the 
change in server utilization distribution before and after 
implementation. Before implementation, server utilization 
showed a bimodal distribution, with many servers operating at 
either very low utilization (10-20%) or very high utilization (80-
90%). After implementation, the distribution shifted toward a

The energy consumption prediction model achieved a Mean 
Absolute Percentage Error (MAPE) of 7.2%, which is sufficient 
for effective capacity planning and optimization. The workload 
energy profiling model demonstrated high precision and recall 
(F1 Score of 0.89) in identifying workloads that could benefit 
from optimization. 
D. Performance Impact 
A critical consideration for any energy optimization 
framework is its impact on application performance and service 
level agreements (SLAs). Table IV summarizes the performance 
metrics before and after implementation. 
Notably, the implementation of the energy optimization 
framework did not negatively impact application performance. 
Most performance metrics showed slight improvements, with 
average response time decreasing by 3.8% and transaction 
throughput increasing by 2.8%. This improvement can be 
attributed to more efficient resource allocation and reduced 
contention on physical hosts. 
The coefficient of variation (CoV) for performance metrics 
decreased by 33.3%, indicating more consistent performance 
with fewer fluctuations. This stability improvement is 
particularly valuable for applications with strict performance 
requirements. 
E. Cost-Benefit Analysis 
 To evaluate the economic viability of the framework, we 
conducted a cost-benefit analysis considering implementation 
costs, energy savings, and operational benefits. Table V presents 
the key financial metrics after implementation of this 
framework. 
The framework demonstrated a strong financial case with a 
return on investment (ROI) of 109% over the six-month 
implementation period and a payback period of 4.8 months. 
Energy cost savings accounted for approximately 30% of the total 
benefits, with deferred capital expenditure (due to improved 
resource utilization) representing the largest benefit category. 
V. CONCLUSION 
This paper presented a comprehensive AIOps framework for 
energy-efficient IT operations in cloud computing environments. 
The framework integrates energy considerations into every 
aspect of IT operations, from data collection and analysis to 
automated optimization. Through a case study implementation in 
a mid-sized cloud environment, we demonstrated that the 
framework could achieve significant energy savings (27% 
reduction) 
while 
maintaining 
or 
improving 
application 
performance. 
The empirical results validate the effectiveness of our 
approach in addressing the dual challenges of operational 
complexity and environmental sustainability in cloud computing. 
The framework's machine learning models demonstrated high 
accuracy in predicting energy consumption and identifying 
optimization 
opportunities, 
enabling 
proactive 
energy 
management. The positive impact on both energy efficiency and 
application performance highlights the potential for AI-driven 
approaches to reconcile seemingly competing objectives. 
Several key insights emerged from this research : 
1.Energy efficiency and operational performance are not 
inherently conflicting goals; properly designed optimization 
strategies can improve both simultaneously. 
2.The integration of diverse data sources, including 
operational metrics, energy consumption, and environmental 
factors, provides a more comprehensive foundation for 
optimization than traditional siloed approaches. 
3.Machine learning models can effectively capture the 
complex 
relationships between 
workload 
characteristics, 
infrastructure configuration, and energy consumption, enabling 
accurate predictions and targeted optimizations. 
4.A phased implementation approach with continuous 
feedback and refinement is essential for the successful adoption 
of AI-driven energy optimization in production environments 
While our case study demonstrated promising results, several 
limitations and opportunities for future research remain. The 
current implementation focuses primarily on server and cooling 
optimization, 
with 
limited 
consideration 
for 
network 
infrastructure and storage systems. Future work could extend the 
2025 IEEE International Symposium on Future Telecommunication Technologies (SOFTT)

framework to encompass these additional components and their 
energy implications. 
 
Additionally, the framework could be enhanced with 
more sophisticated reinforcement learning algorithms that 
continuously adapt optimization strategies based on changing 
conditions and requirements. Integration with renewable energy 
forecasting and carbon intensity data could further improve 
environmental outcomes by aligning workloads with periods of 
clean energy availability.

TITLE:   Optimizing Mutual Fund Portfolio Management through the Application of Advanced Soft Computing Techniques
AUTHORS: ['Jahnavi Ma', 'Kathari Santosh', 'N. Nagasubba Reddy', 'B. Sireesha', 'M. Sandeep Kumar', 'B. Kiran Balad']
YEAR:    2024   VENUE: 2024 Third International Conference on Electrical, Electronics, Information and Communication Technologies (ICEEICT)
DOI:     10.1109/ICEEICT61591.2024.10718611   ARXIV: None
TAGS:    ['portfolio-optimization', 'quantum-ml-finance']
-- ABSTRACT --
This paper proposes an integrated quantum-inspired framework combining a Quantum Neural Network (QNN) for performance prediction with a Quantum-Inspired Evolutionary Algorithm (QEA) for portfolio optimization to improve mutual fund management. Empirical results reported show improved forecast accuracy and decision-making versus traditional methods (mean MAPE 5.45% vs 5.68%), suggesting the approach can enhance risk-adjusted returns and responsiveness to market dynamics.
-- METHOD --
The authors propose an integrated, quantum-inspired framework that combines a Quantum-Inspired Evolutionary Algorithm (QEA) for portfolio optimization with a Quantum Neural Network (QNN) for portfolio performance prediction. The workflow begins with data collection of firm-level financial attributes (e.g., growth %, total funding, valuation, revenue, growth rates, and historical returns for a set of companies) and proceeds with data pre-processing: outlier detection/removal using the interquartile range (IQR) rule, min-max normalization to scale features to [0,1], and missing-value imputation (mean/median imputation is mentioned). The QEA is used to explore portfolio allocation configuration

Abstract—The effective management of mutual fund portfolios 
is paramount for investors seeking to optimize returns while 
minimizing risk. However, traditional optimization techniques 
often struggle to accurately forecast portfolio performance, 
leading to suboptimal investment decisions. To address this 
challenge, this paper offers a novel solution to this problem by 
combining the Quantum Neural Network (QNN) for performance 
prediction with the Quantum-Inspired Evolutionary Algorithm 
(QEA) for portfolio optimisation. Building a flexibl
-- RESULTS WINDOW 1 [1454:4154] --
fectively 
searches the solution space to find the best possible portfolio 
configurations. The suggested integrated framework outperforms 
conventional approaches, as evidenced by empirical testing 
results, which show a mean Absolute Percentage Error (MAPE) of 
5.45% versus 5.68% for Traditional approaches. This indicates 
enhanced decision-making ability and forecast accuracy made 
possible by the integrated method. One potential path towards 
transforming mutual fund portfolio management is the 
implementation of quantum-inspired strategies. This novel 
paradigm has the potential to transform portfolio management 
techniques by providing investors with improved risk-adjusted 
returns and optimized investment strategies under turbulent 
market situations. 
Keywords— Mutual Fund, Portfolio Management, Quantum-
Inspired Algorithms, Quantum-Inspired Evolutionary Algorithm 
(QEA), Quantum Neural Network (QNN), Financial Markets 
I. INTRODUCTION 
Mutual fund portfolio management is a critical aspect of the 
financial industry, aiming to balance risk and return to achieve 
optimal 
investment 
outcomes 
[1]. 
Effective 
portfolio 
management involves selecting the right mix of assets, 
diversifying investments to mitigate risk, and continually 
adjusting the portfolio to respond to market changes [2]. This 
process not only maximizes returns for investors but also ensures 
the stability and growth of investment funds. Given the vast 
amount of capital managed through mutual funds, even small 
improvements in portfolio management strategies can have 
significant financial implications for individual investors and the 
broader market [3]. Traditional portfolio optimization methods 
[4], such as the Markowitz Mean-Variance Optimization 
(MVO) [5] and the Capital Asset Pricing Model (CAPM) [6], 
have been foundational in finance. However, these methods 
come with inherent limitations. The MVO approach, while 
pioneering, often relies on assumptions of normal distribution of 
returns and static market conditions, which do not hold true in 
real-world scenarios. Additionally, it can be computationally 
intensive and may not scale well with the increasing complexity 
and size of modern financial markets [7]. CAPM, on the other 
hand, assumes a linear relationship between expected return and 
risk, which oversimplifies the multi-faceted nature of financial 
markets. Both models are often constrained by their reliance on 
historical data, making them less responsive to real-time market 
dynamics and new information [8]. The advent of quantum 
computing has introduced novel approaches to complex 
problem-solving, particularly in optimization and predictive 
modelin
-- RESULTS WINDOW 2 [8374:14037] --
 Algorithm (QEA) and Quantum Neural 
Network (QNN) would increase risk-adjusted returns for 
investors by streamlining optimisation, cutting down on 
computing 
time, 
and 
providing 
more 
accurate 
performance forecasts. 
 
 
The remaining portions of the paper are arranged as follows: 
Related work is discussed in Section 2. In Section 3, the 
suggested technique is explained. The experimental findings are 
reported and compared in Section 4. In Section 5, further work 
is mentioned and the study is concluded. 
II. LITERATURE REVIEW 
Investors often avoid the risky stock market, preferring 
mutual funds. This study proposes a data mining strategy using 
curve fitting and regression to estimate stock prices, aiming for 
higher profits with lower risks through sector and company 
diversification. Experiments show the framework outperforms 
Indian mutual funds, though maintaining consistent correlation 
coefficients in real data is challenging. [11]. The rise of FinTech 
in China has led to innovations like robo-advisors in wealth 
management, with machine learning, particularly deep learning, 
increasingly applied to financial problems. This paper enhances 
mutual fund classification accuracy and timeliness using a 
Gaussian hybrid clustering algorithm and predicts fund price 
movements with a deep learning model. Testing on 3,625 
Chinese mutual funds shows high accuracy and efficiency, with 
the 
spatiotemporal 
ensemble 
deep 
learning 
module 
outperforming baseline models. The primary contribution is a 
novel method for fund classification and price prediction, aiding 
AI-assisted robo-advisors. Limitations include reliance on 
historical data and the high computational demands of deep 
learning models. [12]. Four machine learning methods are 
applied to predict cross-sectional returns for hedge fund 
selection, using idiosyncratic features derived from historical 
returns. The forecast model significantly outperforms Hedge 
Fund Research indices, with deep neural networks proving most 
effective. Cross-sectional forecasts generally surpass time series 
regression, particularly when combining return-based and macro 
derivative features. Limitations include reliance on historical 
data, which may not fully capture future market conditions, and 
the substantial computational resources required for advanced 
modeling [13]. This study compares machine learning 
techniques, such as boosted regression trees, to traditional 
regression models for predicting mutual fund performance. 
Machine learning significantly enhances prediction power and 
accuracy, setting a new standard for out-of-sample return 
prediction. It identifies key predictors and their relationships 
with performance measures. Limitations include the reliance on 
historical data, which may not fully reflect future market 
conditions, and the substantial computational resources required 
[14].

for

portfolio allocation by leveraging principles from quantum 
computing and evolutionary strategies. Through iterative 
generations and evolutionary operators such as selection, 
mutation, and crossover, the QEA efficiently explores the 
solution space to identify optimal or near-optimal portfolio 
configurations. Based on previous financial data and market 
factors, the QNN is designed to forecast portfolio performance

more educated investment decisions based on real-time market 
data. The integration of the QEA and QNN creates a dynamic 
and adaptive framework for mutual fund portfolio management, 
offering potential improvements in efficiency, accuracy, and 
risk-adjusted returns. This proposed methodology holds promise 
for revolutionizing portfolio management practices by 
harnessing the power of quantum-inspired techniques to

A. Data Collection 
Building an investing portfolio with a variety of firms is the 
main objective of this endeavour. Data parameters like growth 
%, total funding, and valuation will be taken into consideration 
while making the pick. The objective is to create a diversified 
portfolio that maximizes potential for attractive returns while 
controlling risk by utilizing these data sources. The portfolio 
would be constructed with the investor's risk tolerance and 
investing goals in consideration, trying to achieve a balance 
between exposure to firm performance swings and possible 
rewards. It seeks to construct a portfolio that is in line with the 
investor's financial objectives and risk tolerance via careful 
research and smart allocation [15]. 
B. Data Pre-processing 
1) Outlier Detection and Removal: Interquartile Range 
(IQR) 
Identifying and eliminating outliers is an essential part of 
pre-processing data to guarantee the dataset's dependability and 
quality. The robust statistical measure of data dispersion known 
as the Interquartile Range (IQR) [16] could be a useful tool in

utation technique is employed. 
C. Integration of QEA and QNN for Mutual Fund Portfolio 
Management Portfolio Optimization and Prediction 
A potent framework for forecasting and optimising portfolio 
performance in mutual fund management is presented by the 
combination of the Quantum Neural Network (QNN) and the 
Quantum-Inspired Evolutionary Algorithm (QEA). In order to 
create a feedback loop for ongoing development, the QEA and 
QNN communicate seamlessly as part of the integration 
procedure. Through solution space exploration and the 
identification of optimum or nearly optimal portfolio 
configurations, the QEA is in charge of optimising the portfolio 
allocation in this workflow. Utilising concepts from 
evolutionary methods and quantum computing, the QEA finds 
high-performing portfolios quickly while taking investor 
preferences, risk-adjusted returns, and diversification into 
account. The population of portfolio solutions is evolved 
towards improved performance by the QEA using repetitive 
generations and evolutionary operators including crossover, 
mutation, and selection. The QNN, on the other hand, is 
responsible for forecasting portfolio performance using past 
financial data and market indications. In order to improve its 
learning capabilities, the QNN architecture makes utilisation of 
quantum-inspired elements like qubits and quantum gates to 
identify intricate patterns and correlations in the data. Fund 
managers are able to make well-informed investment decisions 
because to the QNN's ability to create predictive models based 
on historical data, which estimate future portfolio returns and 
risks. Through a feedback loop technique, the QEA and QNN 
are integrated. The QNN receives these configurations as input 
for performance prediction from the QEA as it searches the 
solution space and finds viable portfolio combinations. The 
QNN assesses each portfolio configuration's expected 
performance and gives the QEA input on the calibre of these 
solutions. Based on the performance predictions produced by the 
QNN, this feedback loop enables the QEA to modify its search 
strategy and evolutionary parameters. In addition, the optimised 
portfolio results produced by the QEA can teach the QNN new 
things. The QNN could enhance the accuracy of its forecasts for 
future performance by fine-tuning its predictive models and 
adapting to shifting market conditions through the analysis of 
these portfolios' actual performance over time. A dynamic and 
flexible framework for mutual fund portfolio management is 
produced by the combination of the QNN and QEA. This 
technique allows for the continual development and refining of 
portfolio strategies through the combination of prediction and 
optimisation skills in a feedback loop, which eventually 
improves risk-adjusted returns for investors. 
IV. RESULT AND DISCUSSION 
In comparison to conventional portfolio optimisation and 
prediction techniques, the integrated QEA-QNN strategy 
produced better outcomes. Regarding risk-adjusted returns, 
portfolio diversity, and forecast accuracy, the integrated strategy 
continuously beat conventional techniques. This demonstrates 
how well portfolio management procedures and investment 
decision-making processes could be improved through the 
implementation of quantum-inspired methodologies. The study 
was implemented using the Python simulation tool framework. 
The outcomes of combining QNN and QEA show how 
quantum-inspired methods have the power to completely 
transform mutual fund portfolio management.

TITLE:   Design of a Blockchain-Driven Quantum Identity Model Using QHA-AC FQET and RL-QIDA Methods in Cloud Microservices
AUTHORS: ['Venkata Nagarjun A', 'Sujatha Rajkumar', 'Sardar Islam']
YEAR:    2025   VENUE: First International Conference on Intelligent Computing and Systems at the Edge (ICEdge)
DOI:     10.1109/ICEDGE67252.2025.11412890   ARXIV: None
TAGS:    ['cryptography-security']
-- ABSTRACT --
This paper proposes a blockchain-driven, quantum-resistant identity verification framework for microservice-to-microservice authentication in financial cloud infrastructures. It integrates five components—Quantum Hash Anchoring with Adaptive Commitments (QHA-AC), Federated Quantum Entangled Tokens (FQET), Temporal Blockchain Quantum Graph Signatures (TBQGS), Quantum Zero-Knowledge Identity Tree (QZKIT), and Reinforcement Learning-based Quantum Identity Drift Analysis (RL-QIDA)—and reports high identity spoofing detection (up to ~97%), low authentication latency (~1 ms), strong drift detection (~92–95%), and minimal privacy leakage (<0.5%).
-- METHOD --
The authors propose and evaluate a multi-component blockchain-driven quantum-resistant identity verification framework for cloud microservices. They design five methods (QHA-AC, FQET, TBQGS, QZKIT, RL-QIDA) and implement an experimental testbed: a hybrid cloud cluster of 10 virtual microservice nodes, where quantum-entropy simulations are executed on IBM Qiskit backends, the blockchain layer runs on a Hyperledger Fabric testbed, and identity components are containerized with Docker. Identity generation uses Quantum Random Walks to produce temporally-evolving quantum hashes (QHA-AC) anchored on-chain; federated entangled tokens (FQET) distribute entangled key pairs across zones and authentica

V. 
CONCLUSIONS & FUTURE SCOPE 
This study presents a novel, blockchain-driven quantum 
identity verification model for enhancing authentication 
integrity in cloud-native microservice architectures. By 
integrating quantum-resilient cryptographic constructs with 
decentralized trust mechanisms, the proposed framework 
effectively addresses the vulnerabilities of conventional 
identity systems, especially under the emerging quantum 
computing paradigms. The architecture incorporates five 
interlinked 
components—QHA-AC, 
FQET, 
TBQGS, 
QZKIT, and RL-QIDA—each responsible for a distinct 
dimension of identity lifecycle management: entropy-bound 
identity generation, federated authentication, graph-based 
behavior modeling, privacy-preserving validation, and real-
time trust recalibrations. Experimental evaluation across 
three 
representative 
datasets—FINStack-Micro, 
MS-
FinBench, 
and 
CloudAuthSim-Q—demonstrated 
the 
proposed model’s superiority over baseline approaches 
(Method [3], Method [8], Method [12]). Identity spoofing 
detection accuracy reached up to 97.2%, significantly 
surpassing the closest competitor at 85.9%. Authentication 
latency was reduced to 0.96–1.12 ms, achieving a 2.5–4x 
improvement over existing methods. Furthermore, the drift 
detection accuracy of 94.6% and the trust recalibration 
window of just 5 service calls highlight the model’s 
adaptiveness in dynamic service environments. Privacy 
leakage was minimized to 0.39–0.47%, enabled by the zero-
knowledge validation via QZKIT, compared to leakage rates 
above 2.5% in conventional schemes. The use of lightweight 
temporal graph signatures in TBQGS resulted in a blockchain 
consensus timestamp of 11.8–12.6 ms, approximately 
halving the latency relative to other blockchain-based identity 
mechanisms. In conclusion, the model not only addresses the 
immediate limitations of existing edge authentication 
systems but also establishes a scalable, quantum-secure 
foundation for identity management. Its composability, low-
latency performance, and resistance to quantum-enabled 
attacks position it as a forward-compatible solution for 
sectors such as finance, healthcare, and critical infrastructure 
sets.

-- ABSTRACT --
This preprint introduces the Quantum Finance Optimization Qubit (QFOQ), a proposal for specialized qubits designed to handle high-dimensional, noisy financial data and to speed up backtesting and forward testing. The model integrates adaptive qubit states, financial-specific error correction, quantum amplitude amplification, and quantum Monte Carlo methods to claim substantial reductions in computation time and improved prediction accuracy for applications such as algorithmic trading, portfolio optimization, and risk management.
-- METHOD --
The paper proposes a comparative experimental methodology divided into three phases: (1) establish a classical backtesting baseline using high-performance classical hardware and standard financial modeling toolchains; (2) implement the same tests on a quantum-enabled environment using the proposed QFOQ (Quantum Finance Optimization Qubit) architecture to measure execution time and accuracy gains; and (3) perform data analysis and statistical validation. Data collection focuses on historical stock prices, macroeconomic indicators and sentiment data; preprocessing includes normalization, segmentation by market regime (bull, bear, high-volatility) and noise filtering. Quantum Finance Optimization Qubits
(QFOQ): An Approach to Accelerate
Financial Model Testing
Abstract
Quantum Finance Optimization Qubit (QFOQ) model, a novel approach designed to accelerate
backtesting and forward testing in quantitative finance through the development of specialized
qubits. Unlike general-purpose quantum computing applications, QFOQs are tailored to handle
the high-dimensional, noisy nature of financial data. By integrating error correction, adaptive
noise resistance, and quantum optimization, QFOQs offer substantial improvements in
computational speed and model accuracy. This theory suggests that QFOQs can revolutionize
financial model testing, opening new frontiers in algorithmic trading, portfolio optimization, and
risk management.
1. Introduction
Quantitative finance relies heavily on backtesting and forward testing to develop and refine
trading models. These processes are computationally intensive, especially when working with
large datasets under variable market conditions. While classical computing can manage these
tasks, it often struggles with the complexity and speed required for real-time financial
applications. Quantum computing promises significant

results.

○
Platform: High-performance classical computing hardware.
○
Software: Standard financial modeling tools (e.g., Python, R) for running
backtesting and forward testing.
○
Metrics: Execution time and model accuracy are recorded as baseline metrics
for comparison with QFOQ-enhanced tests.
2.
QFOQ-Enabled Quantum Testing:
○
Quantum Platform: A quantum computing environment capable of running
specialized QFOQs. IBM Qiskit or Rigetti's Forest could be used, depending on

○
QFOQ Integration: The QFOQs are deployed to dynamically adjust to the
financial data patterns, and error-correcting mechanisms are applied to handle

○

utilized to increase the selection probability of high-value trading signals.
Quantum Monte Carlo simulations are implemented to enhance scenario testing
efficiency.

○
Execution Time: Total processing time is measured and compared between
classical and quantum setups.
○
Accuracy Assessment: Prediction accuracy is assessed by comparing the
model’s predictive performance across various market conditions and by
measuring error rates.
○
Noise Resilience: Additional experiments are conducted under varying levels of
data noise to assess the effectiveness of QFOQ’s noise-resistant features.
2.5 Data Analysis
●
Execution Time Analysis: Time reduction is calculated as a percentage relative to the
classical baseline, verifying if QFOQs meet the hypothesized 50% improvement in
computational time.
●
Accuracy Metrics: Model accuracy is evaluated using standard error metrics (e.g.,
Mean Absolute Error, Root Mean Square Error) to confirm that QFOQs maintain or
improve accuracy levels.
●
Statistical Validation: Statistical tests (e.g., t-tests) are conducted to validate whether
differences in execution time and accuracy between classical and QFOQ-enabled setups
are statistically significant.
2.6 Model Calibration
To ensure robustness, iterative tests are performed to calibrate QFOQs:
●
Parameter Tuning: Parameters in QFOQ algorithms (e.g., amplification factor, number
of qubits used) are optimized based on performance in early experiments.
●
Noise-Resistant Calibration: Error-correcting protocols are adjusted to maximize the
model’s accuracy under high noise conditions, validating QFOQ’s adaptability to financial
data fluctuations.
2.7 Reliability and Validity
●
Reliability: Consistency in results across multiple runs ensures reliability. Each test is
repeated at least five times to confirm that the observed performance gains are not due
to random variations.
●
External Validity: To ensure the generalizability of findings, the QFOQ model is tested
across different market conditions, including bull, bear, and high-volatility periods. This
assessment guarantees that QFOQs perform reliably across diverse financial
environments.
2.8 Limitations and Considerations
●
Quantum Hardware Limitations: As quantum computing is still developing, hardware
limitations may affect performance outcomes. Thus, results are framed within the context
of current hardware capabilities.


●
Data Sensitivity: Financial data is often sensitive to noise and market conditions, so
while QFOQs are designed to handle noise, varying market dynamics may impact
testing results.

Resource Constraints: Access to quantum hardware for extensive t

6. Practical Implications and Applications in Finance
If validated, QFOQs could transform financial modeling and risk management by enabling
faster, more precise testing processes. They could redefine the role of quantum computing in
algorithmic trading, portfolio optimization, and market risk analysis, offering a computational
advantage that traditional systems cannot match.

●
Risk Mitigation and Loss Prevention: Faster backtesting means strategies can be

TITLE:   Uncertainty and symmetry bounds for the quantum total detection probability
AUTHORS: ['Felix Thiel', 'Itay Mualem', 'David A. Kessler', 'Eli Barkai']
YEAR:    2019   VENUE: arXiv preprint arXiv:1906.08108
DOI:        ARXIV: None
TAGS:    []
-- ABSTRACT --
The paper studies the total detection probability Pdet for discrete quantum systems subject to repeated projective (stroboscopic) measurements to detect a target state |d⟩, showing that unlike classical walks Pdet can be less than one due to destructive interference. It derives a lower bound in the form of an uncertainty relation linking the deviation of Pdet from the initial overlap to energy fluctuations and a commutator, and an upper bound Pdet ≤ 1/ν based on symmetry (ν = number of states equivalent to the initial state), provides an exact expression via decomposition into bright and dark subspaces, and illustrates these results on small graphs and large systems (e.g., hypercube).
-- METHOD --
This is a theoretical and analytical study of repeated projective detection in discrete quantum systems (quantum walks) on finite graphs. The authors model the dynamics as unitary evolution U(τ)=exp(-iτH) interleaved with stroboscopic projective measurements onto a detection state |d⟩ at times τ, 2τ, .... They decompose the system Hilbert space into bright and dark subspaces (HB and HD) using the eigenbasis of H and properties of the measurement projector D=|d⟩⟨d|, and derive an exact expression for the total detection probability Pdet by identifying bright states in each degenerate energy sector and summing their contributions (Eq. (13)). From the collapse postulate they derive an uncertain

Uncertainty and symmetry bounds for the quantum total detection probability
Felix Thiel, Itay Mualem, David A. Kessler, and Eli Barkai
Department of Physics, Institute of Nanotechnology and Advanced Materials, Bar-Ilan University, Ramat-Gan 52900, Israel
We investigate a generic discrete quantum system prepared in state |ψin⟩, under repeated detection
attempts aimed to ﬁnd the particle in state |d⟩, for example a quantum walker on a ﬁnite graph
searching for a node. For the corresponding classical random walk, the total detection probability
Pdet is unity. Due to destructive interference, one may ﬁnd initial states |ψin⟩with Pdet < 1. We
ﬁrst obtain an uncertainty relation which yields insight on this deviation from classical behavior,
showing the relation between Pdet and energy ﬂuctuations: ∆P Var[ ˆH]d ≥| ⟨d|[ ˆH, ˆD] |ψin⟩|

we show that Pdet ≤1/ν where the integer ν is the number of states equivalent to the initial state.
These bounds are compared with the exact solution for small systems, obtained from an analysis of
the dark and bright subspaces, showing the usefulness

Diﬀerent lower bounds for the hypercube

been mentioned in the main text in a hypercube with

(thus best) lower bound.

TITLE:   Quantum and Classical Machine Learning in Decentralized Finance: Comparative Evidence from Multi-Asset Backtesting of Automated Market Makers
AUTHORS: ['Chi-Sheng Chen', 'Aidan Hung-Wen Tsai']
YEAR:    2025   VENUE: arXiv preprint (arXiv:2510.15903v1)
DOI:        ARXIV: None
TAGS:    ['trading-execution', 'quantum-ml-finance']
-- ABSTRACT --
This preprint reports a large-scale empirical comparison of quantum, classical, and hybrid quantum-classical machine learning models for Automated Market Maker (AMM) and DeFi trading strategies using one year of daily crypto data. The authors find hybrid quantum-classical models (notably a QASA Sequence variant) outperform pure quantum and classical baselines on average, achieving the highest individual return (13.99%) and best risk-adjusted performance (Sharpe 1.76), while classical ensemble methods remain strong and computationally efficient. The study highlights current limitations of pure quantum models and recommends hybrid approaches for practical DeFi applications.
-- METHOD --
The study performs a large-scale empirical comparison of classical, pure-quantum, hybrid quantum-classical, and transformer models for AMM/DeFi trading decisions via backtesting on daily cryptocurrency data over the 2024 calendar year. Targets are binary rebalancing/trade signals defined by task-specific threshold rules (AMM rebalance, concentrated-liquidity position, quantum-enhanced price move). Extensive feature engineering is applied: classical pipelines use rich feature sets (50–80 features; 122 features enumerated in the appendix) including price, moving averages, technical indicators, volatility, volume, time, microstructure proxies, lags and interactions; quantum pipelines use compac

overall performance with 11.2% average return and
1.42 average Sharpe ratio, while classical ML models
show 9.8% average return and 1.47 average Sharpe
ratio. The QASA Sequence hybrid model achieves
the highest individual return of 13.99% with the best
Sharpe ratio of 1.76, demonstrating the potential of

IV. Experiments
A. Overall Performance Comparison
The results in Table II show clear performance dif-
ferences across model families. Classical machine learn-
ing methods achieved strong and balanced results, with
a 9.8% average return and the highest Sharpe ratio
(1.47). Pure quantum models underperformed in both
returns (4.4%) and risk-adjusted performance (Sharpe
0.83), while also exhibiting higher volatility. Hybrid quan-
tum approaches provided a strong trade-off, delivering
the highest average return among the quantum-based
methods (11.2%) and relatively low volatility (11.0%),
with performance close to classical models. Transformer-
based models yielded the best overall returns (12.3%)
but at the cost of elevated volatility (15.4%), leading to
a lower Sharpe ratio compared with classical methods.
These findings suggest that hybrid designs can combine
quantum advantages with classical stability, whereas pure
quantum models currently lag behind.
B. Asset-Specific Analysis
1) Individual Model Performance Analysis: The per-
formance ranking of individual models highlights the ad-
vantages of hybrid quantum-classical approaches. Among
all tested models, QASA Sequence achieved the highest
return (13.99%), Sharpe ratio (1.76), and Calmar ratio
(6.51), clearly outperforming both classical and purely
quantum baselines. Classical ensemble methods such as
Random Forest (13.16% return, Sharpe 1.68) and Gradi-
ent Boosting (12.31% return, Sharpe 1.68) also delivered
strong results, though with lower risk-adjusted metrics
compared to QASA. Transformer-based models showed
competitive returns (11.73%) but lower Sharpe (1.23)
and Calmar (1.64), indicating higher volatility exposure.
Other hybrid quantum models like QASA Hybrid (11.91%
return) and QuantumRWKV (7.96% return) achieved
moderate performance, while purely quantum methods
(QSVM, QNN, VQE Classifier) underperformed across
all metrics, reflecting current limitations of standalone
quantum approaches. Overall, these findings underscore
that hybrid quantum-classical designs currently offer the
best balance between profitability and risk management
in financial trading tasks.
Key Findings:
• QASA Sequence achieves highest return (13.99%)
with best Sharpe ratio (1.76)
• Classical ML models (Random Forest, Gradient
Boosting) show strong performance with high Sharpe
ratios (1.68)
• QuantumRWKV demonstrates balanced performance

2) Model Type Performance Analysis: The comparison
across model types reveals distinct trade-offs between
return, risk, and volatility. Transformer models achieved
the highest average return (12.3%) but also exhibited
elevated volatility (15.4%) and the deepest drawdowns (-
8.2%). Hybrid quantum models offered a more balanced
profile, combining strong returns (11.2%) with the lowest
volatility (11.0%) and relatively shallow drawdowns (-
3.3%), highlighting their effectiveness in risk management.
Classical ML methods achieved moderate returns (9.8%)
with the best Sharpe ratio (1.47), reflecting stable but
less aggressive performance. In contrast, pure quantum
models significantly underperformed (4.4% return, 0.83
Sharpe) while incurring the highest volatility (17.1%), un-
derscoring the current limitations of standalone quantum
approaches. These findings suggest that hybrid quantum-
classical models offer the most favorable balance of prof-
itability and stability, outperforming both purely classical
and purely quantum strategies.
Key Findings:
• Hybrid Quantum models achieve highest returns
(11.2%) with best risk management (lowest volatility
11.0%)
• Classical ML models show strong performance with
high Sharpe ratio (1.47) and moderate volatility
(13.3%)
• Transformer models achieve good returns (12.3%) but
with higher volatility (15.4%) and drawdown (-8.2%)
• Pure Quantum models show lowest performance with
poor returns (4.4%) and highest volatility (17.1%)
• Hybrid models demonstrate superior risk-adjusted
performance with lowest average drawdown (-3.3%)
C. Risk-Return Analysis
The risk-return analysis shows clear differences across
models. QASA Sequence delivered the strongest over-
all performance, combining the highest return (13.99%)
and Sharpe ratio (1.76) with relatively low volatility
(8.35%). Classical ensemble models such as Random For-
est and Gradient Boosting also achieved competitive re-
turns (13.16% and 12.31%, respectively) but with higher
volatility and drawdowns. QASA Hybrid provided bal-
anced risk control, maintaining solid returns (11.91%)
while minimizing drawdowns (-1.70%). Transformer mod-
els produced strong returns (11.73%) but suffered from the
highest volatility among top performers (15.39%). In con-
trast, purely quantum models (QSVM, QNN, VQE Classi-
fier) underperformed, showing low returns (3–5%), weaker
Sharpe ratios (<0.9), and elevated volatility, underscoring
current limitations of standalone quantum approaches.
Overall, these results highlight that hybrid quantum-
classical models—especially QASA variants—offer the
most favorable balance of profitability, stability, and risk
management.
Key Findings:
• QASA Sequence shows best risk-adjusted returns
(highest Sharpe ratio 1.76) with lowest volatility
(8.35%)

D. Uncertainty and Robustness Analysis
Our experimental design includes multiple runs (5 runs
per model) to assess model uncertainty and robustness.
The analysis reveals significant differences in model sta-
bility across different architectures.
The uncertainty analysis, measured by standard de-
viation across five runs, highlights differences in model
stability. QASA Sequence achieved top stability ranking,
though with higher variance in return (2.04%) and Sharpe
(0.26), reflecting sensitivity to training dynamics despite
strong average performance. Classical ensemble models
such as Random Forest and Gradient Boosting demon-
strated consistently low variability, securing high stabil-
ity ranks (2 and 3). QASA Hybrid balanced moderate
return variability (1.04%) with robust stability (rank 4).
Transformer and hybrid recurrent models (e.g., Quantum-
RWKV) exhibited relatively low volatility fluctuations,
placing them mid-range in stability. In contrast, purely
quantum approaches (QNN, VQE Classifier) showed the
highest volatility uncertainty (2.61% and 3.10%) and
ranked lowest in stability, reinforcing the current limi-
tations of standalone quantum strategies. Overall, these
findings confirm that hybrid and classical ensemble meth-
ods provide the most reliable performance consistency,
while pure quantum models remain less stable.
Key Findings:
• QASA Sequence shows highest performance variabil-
ity but maintains top performance
• Classical ML models (Random Forest, Gradient
Boosting) show moderate variability with consistent
high performance
• QuantumRWKV
demonstrates
excellent
stability
with low variability across all metrics
• Pure quantum models show high volatility variability,
indicating unstable performance
• Transformer models show good stability with consis-
tent performance across runs
E. Model Complexity and Efficiency Analysis
The complexity–efficiency analysis reveals important
trade-offs between model design and computational cost.
QASA Sequence demonstrated the highest complexity (8)
and long training time (6.0 units), but its efficiency (1.30)
and stability (0.95) justify the overhead given its strong re-
turns. In contrast, Random Forest and Gradient Boosting
offered the most favorable balance, with low complexity
(3–4), minimal training time (1.0–2.0), and the highest ef-
ficiency scores (1.66 and 1.61, respectively). QASA Hybrid
achieved a good compromise between reduced complexity
(6) and strong stability (0.98), though at a lower efficiency
(0.90). Transformer models maintained solid stability but
incurred high training cost relative to their efficiency. Pure
quantum models (QSVM, QNN, VQE Classifier) showed
limited efficiency (<0.5) despite moderate complexity
levels, underscoring scalability challenges. Overall, these


Fig. 5. Risk return scatter of all models.
findings highlight that classical ensemble methods remain
the most computationally efficient, while hybrid models
like QASA provide a viable balance between complexity,
stability, and performance gains.
Key Findings:
• QASA Sequence shows highest complexity but best
efficiency score despite high variability
• Classical ML models show good efficiency with lower
complexity and high stability
• QuantumRWKV
demonstrates
excellent
stability
with moderate complexity
• Pure quantum models show poor efficiency despite
high complexity and high variability
• Training time correlates with model complexity, with
hybrid models requiring more computational re-
sources
F. Hybrid Model Advantage Scenarios
1) QASA Sequence Model Superiority: QASA Sequence
model achieves the best overall performance due to:
1) LSTM Temporal Processing: Long Short-Term
Memory networks capture sequential patterns in
financial time series data, providing better context
for decision making.
2) Quantum Enhancement: Quantum layers process
the LSTM outputs to capture nonlinear relation-
ships that classical methods might miss.
3) Hybrid Architecture: The combination of clas-
sical sequence processing and quantum feature en-
hancement provides complementary advantages.
2) QASA Hybrid Model Performance: QASA Hybrid
model shows strong performance due to:
1) Balanced Approach: Classical layers provide sta-
bility while quantum layers add pattern recognition
capabilities.
2) Risk
Management:
The
hybrid
architecture
achieves the lowest maximum drawdown (-1.83%),
indicating superior risk control.
3) Efficiency: Moderate complexity (6) with good
efficiency score (0.90) makes it practical for deploy-
ment.

G. Classical ML Advantage Scenarios
1) High Accuracy and Stability: Classical ML models
excel in accuracy and stability due to:
1) High Accuracy: Random Forest and Gradient
Boosting achieve 99.48% accuracy, demonstrating
superior pattern recognition in training data.
2) Training Efficiency: Classical ML models train
much faster (0.5-2.0 time units) compared to quan-
tum models (4.0-6.0 time units).
3) Feature
Richness:

ML
can
utilize
more features (50-80), performing better in high-
dimensional feature spaces.
4) Proven Reliability: Well-established algorithms
with extensive optimization and tuning capabilities.
H. Pure Quantum Model Challenges
Pure quantum models (VQE Classifier, QNN, QSVM)
show significant challenges:
1) Low Accuracy: All pure quantum models achieve
less than 50% accuracy, indicating poor pattern
recognition.


2) Poor Returns: Pure quantum models show the
lowest returns (3.18% - 5.68%) among all model
types.
3) High Volatility: Quantum models exhibit high
volatility (15.47% - 19.89%), leading to poor risk-
adjusted returns.
4) Training Challenges: Longer training times (4.0-
5.0 time units) with poor efficiency scores (0.31-
0.41).
V. Conclusions and Recommendations
A. Main Conclusions
1) Hybrid quantum-classical models achieve best
performance: QASA Sequence model achieves the
highest return (13.99%) with the best Sharpe ratio
(1.76) and superior risk management, demonstrat-
ing the potential of quantum-classical hybrid ap-
proaches.
2) Classical ML provides strong and stable per-
formance: Random Forest and Gradient Boosting
achieve excellent returns (12.31% - 13.16%) with
high Sharpe ratios (1.68) and consistent perfor-
mance across multiple runs.
3) Pure quantum models show significant lim-
itations: All pure quantum models (VQE, QNN,
QSVM) achieve poor returns (3.00% - 4.77%) and
low Sharpe ratios (0.79 - 0.87), indicating current
limitations in practical applications.
4) QuantumRWKV demonstrates balanced per-
formance: The QuantumRWKV model shows ex-
cellent stability with moderate returns (7.96%) and
good Sharpe ratio (1.19), suggesting potential for
quantum-enhanced sequence models.
5) Uncertainty analysis reveals model robust-
ness: Multiple runs analysis shows that hybrid mod-
els, while achieving top performance, exhibit higher
variability, while classical models provide more con-
sistent results.
B. Practical Recommendations
1) Model Selection Strategy:
• Maximum return optimization: Use QASA Se-
quence model for highest returns (13.99%) with best
risk-adjusted performance (Sharpe 1.76).
• Stable high performance: Use Random Forest
or Gradient Boosting for consistent high returns
(12.31% - 13.16%) with excellent stability.
• Balanced performance: Use QASA Hybrid model
for good returns (11.91%) with excellent risk control
(lowest drawdown -1.70%).
• Stable moderate performance: Use QuantumR-
WKV for consistent moderate returns (7.96%) with
excellent stability across runs.
• Real-time trading: Use classical ML models (Ran-
dom Forest, Gradient Boosting) for fast response and
consistent performance.
• Avoid pure quantum models: Current pure quan-
tum models show poor performance (3.00% - 4.77%
returns) and are not recommended for practical ap-
plications.
2) Feature Engineering Recommendations:
• Classical ML: Use rich feature sets (50-80 features)
including basic features, technical indicators, and
interaction features for maximum accuracy.
• Hybrid models: Use moderate feature sets (12 fea-
tures) with classical preprocessing and quantum angle
encoding for optimal balance.
• Pure quantum models: Current limitations sug-
gest avoiding pure quantum approaches until signifi-
cant improvements are achieved.
• Feature selection: Focus on price ratios, volatil-
ity measures, and 
-- RESULTS WINDOW 3 [32399:35119] --
test) ensured consistency and
robustness across experiments.

TITLE:   Unveiling the Quantum Realm: A Comprehensive Analysis of Quantum Computing
AUTHORS: ['Salma Mostafa A. Helmy', 'Salsabil A. El-Regaily', 'Abeer M. Mahmoud', 'El-Sayed M. El-Horbaty']

TAGS:    ['portfolio-optimization', 'simulation-monte-carlo', 'cryptography-security']
-- ABSTRACT --
This paper presents a comprehensive overview of quantum computing, covering theoretical foundations (qubits, superposition, entanglement, measurement), physical architectures (superconducting, trapped ions, photonics), quantum gates and key algorithms, and the milestone of quantum advantage. It surveys potential applications across industries including drug discovery, materials science, cryptography, and financial modeling, and discusses major challenges such as decoherence, scalability, and the need for robust quantum error correction to achieve fault-tolerant quantum computers.
-- METHOD --
This work is a non‑empirical, comprehensive literature synthesis and comparative analysis of quantum computing. The authors survey foundational theory (qubits, superposition, entanglement, measurement), map those concepts to contemporary hardware and software architectures, and assemble vendor‑level comparative benchmarks (presented as tables) and roadmap visualizations. The methodology relies on critical review of prior literature (textbooks, seminal papers, review articles) together with recent vendor technical updates and reports (IBM, IonQ, Rigetti) to: (1) synthesize theoretical foundations with physical implementations, (2) compare platform trade‑offs (qubit counts, coherence times, ga

Abstract—Quantum computing represents a paradigm shift 
from classical computation, harnessing the principles of 
quantum mechanics to process information in fundamentally 
new ways. This paper provides a comprehensive analysis of the 
field, beginning with its theoretical underp

qubits can represent 2^N 
states simultaneously. This exponential scaling underpins the 
potential of quantum computing to solve problems otherwise 
intractable on classical machines as illustrate in table 1.

C. Superposition: A Multi-State Existence 
 
Superposition allows qubits to exist in multiple states until 
measured, akin to a spinning coin that represents both heads 
and tails before it lands. With N qubits, a quantum system can 
occupy a superposition of 2^N possible states. Algorithms 
exploit this property through quantum parallelism, applying 
operations across all states simultaneously. 
However, quantum algorithms must ensure that when the 
system is measured, the probability distribution favors correct 
results. This is achieved by interference: undesired outcomes 
destructively interfere while correct outcomes constructively 
reinforce [3]. Thus, algorithm design requires carefully 
orchestrating superpositions to maximize useful results[23]. 
 
 
 
 
D. Entanglement: “Spooky Action at a Distance” 
 
Entanglement, described by Einstein as “spooky action at a 
distance,” is a unique quantum phenomenon where two or 
more qubits share correlations regardless of spatial 
separation. For example, in the Bell state (|00⟩ + |11⟩)/√2, (1) 
neither qubit has a definite state, but their outcomes are 
perfectly correlated. 
Entanglement enables non-classical correlations vital for 
quantum information processing. Multi-qubit gates such as 
the CNOT generate entanglement and form the backbone of 
quantum circuits. This phenomenon is central to the 
efficiency of Shor’s factoring algorithm and Grover’s search 
algorithm, which rely on such correlations for computational 
speedups[21]. 
 
 
 
Authorized licensed use limited to: CBS Library. Downloaded on March 31,2026 at 20:35:03 UTC from IEEE Xplore. Restrictions apply. 


E. Quantum Measurement and Decoherence 
 
Measurement collapses a qubit into a definite classical 
outcome, eliminating superposition. Since full quantum states 
cannot be directly observed, algorithms must be repeated to 
sample probability distributions. 
Decoherence represents a major challenge. Environmental 
interactions act as unintended measurements, destroying 
fragile superposition and entanglement [1]. Coherence time 
defines how long a qubit can resist decoherence. Engineering 
progress focuses on extending coherence times and 
implementing error correction techniques to protect qubits 
from environmental noise. Without overcoming decoherence, 
the theoretical power of quantum computing remains 
unattainable[22]. 
 
III. 
Architecture and Operation of Quantum Computers 
 
Quantum computers embody these theoretical principles in 
hardware and operational frameworks. Their architecture 
requires 
novel 
implementations 
for 
qubits, 
control 
mechanisms, and error mitigation. Central aspects include 
physical realization of qubits, quantum gates, algorithms, and 
milestones like quantum advantage. 
A. Comparative Analysis of Quantum Hardware 
Vendors 
 
The commercial landscape is defined by key vendors 
leveraging different physical platforms, leading to a 
trade-off in performance metrics such as qubit count and 
coherence time. For example, major players like IBM 
(Superconducting), IonQ (Trapped Ions), and Rigetti 
(Superconducting) are at the forefront of development, 
each emphasizing a different strength in their 
architecture , as illustrate in table 2.

These benchmarks illustrate the physical trade-offs: 
Superconducting platforms like IBM prioritize large-scale 
integration (high qubit count) but struggle with coherence 
time, while Trapped-Ion systems like IonQ offer superior 
fidelity and coherence but currently lag in raw qubit numbers. 
The race is to find the optimal balance among these metrics 
to achieve fault tolerance. 
 
B. Physical Implementations of Qubits 
 
Unlike standardized silicon transistors, qubits can be realized 
in multiple physical forms. Common approaches include 
superconducting circuits, trapped ions, and photonic systems, 
each with strengths and weaknesses. 
Superconducting qubits, fabricated from materials like 
aluminum or niobium and cooled to cryogenic temperatures, 
represent one of the most mature platforms. They benefit 
from 
fast 
gate 
operations 
and 
compatibility 
with 
semiconductor manufacturing, making them scalable. 
However, coherence times remain short due to sensitivity to 
noise [3]. 
Trapped-ion qubits exploit the stable electronic energy levels 
of ions suspended in electromagnetic traps. They achieve 
exceptionally long coherence times and high-fidelity gates 
[6]. Their limitation lies in slower gate speeds and the 
difficulty of scaling up large arrays of ions. 
Photonic qubits encode information in properties of photons 
such as polarization. They are robust against decoherence and 
suitable for quantum communication. However, challenges 
include reliable single-photon sources and efficient two-
photon interactions. 
Other platforms, including neutral atoms and quantum dots, 
are also being pursued. Each presents trade-offs among 
scalability, stability, and operational complexity, and hybrid 
architectures may ultimately combine multiple modalities. 

-- RESULTS WINDOW 2 [23367:26067] --
lerant quantum computers capable of 
transformative impact. 
Global collaboration among academia, industry, and 
governments will be vital for overcoming scientific and 
engineering barriers. 
 
VI. 
Conclusion 
 
Quantum computing departs from classical paradigms by 
leveraging superposition, entanglement, and measurement. 
These phenomena enable exponential parallelism and unique 
computational correlations that power algorithms such as 
Shor’s and Grover’s. 
Applications span medicine, materials science, cybersecurity, 
and finance. Yet realizing this promise requires overcoming 
decoherence, error correction, and scalability challenges. The 
current results, exemplified by the rapid growth in qubit count 
and the demonstration of quantum advantage, confirm the 
fundamental feasibility of the technology, but the 
heterogeneous hardware landscape (Superconducting vs. 
Trapped Ion systems) illustrates that no single platform has 
yet achieved the optimal balance of scale and fidelity for 
universal fault-tolerant computation. The necessity of the 
QEC threshold theorem means current efforts must intensely 
focus on increasing gate fidelity alongside qubit count.The 
NISQ era represents an exploratory phase where noisy 
hardware is tested for practical value as represent in figure 2. 
 
 
 
Fig. 2. A generalized quantum roadmap schematic depicting the future 
trajectory of quantum computing. The top graph shows the projected 
exponential growth in the number of qubits, with key milestones such as the 
"Quantum advantage Demo" and the eventual advent of "Commercial 
Quantum Computers" noted along the timeline. The bottom graph illustrates 
the critical trend of decreasing error rates (decoherence) necessary for 
achieving large-scale, fault-tolerant quantum systems. 
 
The long-term vision is a fault-tolerant universal quantum 
computer. 
Achieving 
it 
demands 
interdisciplinary 
collaboration across physics, engineering, and computer 
science. While the path forward is uncertain, the 
transformative potential of quantum computing ensures it will 
remain a central focus of twenty-first-century technological 
progress.

TITLE:   Enhanced Ransomware Detection using a Hybrid Quantum-Classical Convolutional Neural Network with Boosted Sooty Tern Optimization
AUTHORS: ['Chaithanya B N', 'Renuka R. Patil']
YEAR:    2025   VENUE: Proceedings of the Fourth International Conference on Sentiment Analysis and Deep Learning (ICSADL-2025)
DOI:     10.1109/ICSADL65848.2025.10933393   ARXIV: None
TAGS:    ['cryptography-security']
-- ABSTRACT --
This paper proposes a multi-stage ransomware detection framework combining correlation-coefficient based min-max normalization for preprocessing, a hybrid Osprey Optimization with an enhanced Sparrow Search Algorithm for feature selection, and a hybrid quantum-classical convolutional neural network optimized by Boosted Sooty Tern Optimization. Evaluated on CICIDS2017 and mixed ransomware traffic, the method reports very high performance (claimed accuracy ~99.7%, F1 ~99.5%) with low error rate and short computational time, arguing suitability for real-time deployment.
-- METHOD --
The study proposes a multi-stage ransomware-detection pipeline that combines classical preprocessing, metaheuristic feature selection, and a hybrid quantum-classical convolutional neural network optimized by a bio-inspired optimizer. Input traffic labeled from CICIDS2017 and ransomware captures is first preprocessed using a Correlation Coefficient-based Min–Max Normalization (CCMMN) step: features are min–max scaled to [0,1], Pearson correlation coefficients to the label are computed, and features are weighted according to those correlations. Feature selection is then performed by a hybrid metaheuristic (OOA-ESSA) that fuses the Osprey Optimization Algorithm (OOA) for exploration with an enh

Abstract— Ransomware attacks threaten organizations by 
encrypting files or locking systems and keeping them 
inaccessible unless a ransom is paid. Early detection of 
ransomware attacks helps organizations avoid financial losses, 
theft of data, and harm to their reputations. This early 
detection will also reduce the risks related to malicious 
activities. Detection by signature-based or heuristic approaches 
tends to be traditional and fails when evolvement in 
ransomware attacks is considered. Thus, a need for innovative 
techniques of deep learning and optimization algorithms can 
be observed. The method proposed in this study focuses on 
developing a novel multi-stage framework for detecting 
ransomware attack
-- RESULTS WINDOW 1 [9387:15394] --
; instead, it employs a 
machine learning classifier to collect data for certain 
processor and disk I/O operations. The random forest 
classifier performed better than all other classifiers, giving 
results with an accuracy of 98% in the worst-case scenario, 
identifying the ransomware within 400 ms across the six 
user loads and 22 ransomware incidences. The only 
drawback associated with the suggested method is on I/O 
events on the CPU and disk. 
Moreira et al. (2023) [11] have shown an efficient 
approach to ransomware detection that employs an 
improved Xception CNN algorithm based on portable 
executable headers. Crypto-ransomware is a special kind of 
malware that threatens computer systems, and its usage has 
increased due to the COVID-19 pandemic. A method called 
Xception ColSeq converts the PE header files into color 
images and then uses an Xception Convolutional Neural 
Network model to classify these images to identify 
ransomware through static analysis. It can be applied to 
enhancing the efficiency of existing approaches in parallel 
with the protection of computer systems from cyber threats. 
The suggested method has a disadvantage, which is its 
applicability to Portable Executable (PE) files only. 
A. Problem Statement 
Currently, it is challenging to identify ransomware 
attacks because of poor preprocessing of these attacks, 
wrong choice of features, low accuracy, and high 
computational 
complexity.

improvement 
strategy 
that 
incorporates

incorporates the BSTO for parameters of optimality and 
achieves better results in the identification of attack activity 
with low computation complexity. This goes a long way in 
ensuring the improvement of the underlying problems with 
detecting ransomware attacks.

 a. Boosted Sooty Tern Optimization 
Onychoprion fuscatus, commonly referred to as 
sooty terns, is a species of seabird. As omnivores, they 
consume a wide range of creatures, including fish, 
amphibians, insects, reptiles, and earthworms. They are 
intelligent, colony organisms that find and hunt prey. 
Seasonally migrating in search of plentiful food supplies, 
sooty terns congregate to prevent collisions. For air attacks, 
they employ a flapping mode while in flight, adjusting their 
starting positions according to the sooty tern that is the 
fittest. The application of BSTO results in effective loss 
function minimization [16]. BSTO is used to minimize the 
loss function of HQCCNN. The fitness function is given in 
equation (12).

The proposed method involves data acquisition, 
preprocessing 
through 
correlation 
coefficients 
and 
normalization, and feature selection using a hybrid Osprey 
optimization algorithm and an enhancing sparrow search 
algorithm. The features are then fed into a quantum-classical 
convolutional neural network, optimized using boosted 
sooty tern optimization for attack detection. 
IV. RESULTS AND DISCUSSION 
In terms of accuracy, precision, and recall, the 
suggested approach performs better on the dataset than the 
traditional models. To improve stability and dependability, 
pre-processing, 
feature 
selection, 
and 
optimization 
procedures are combined. The method works well across the 
dataset and has greater detection accuracy.

The effectiveness of the suggested strategy is

proposed method has been compared with well-known 
methods such as Xception ColSeq [11], EDVT-MSSAN [9],

several key performance metrics, including F1 score, 
specificity, recall, accuracy, and precision, to demonstrate 
the efficacy of the proposed method at detecting.

Figure 2 is a bar chart comparing the accuracy and 
F1 score of a classification model across different 
categories: Normal, GandCrab, Shade, Cryptowall, Cerber, 
WannaCry, Locky, and Teslacrypt. Every category has two 
bars, one containing the accuracy value (indicated in purple) 
and the second containing the F1-score value (indicated in 
green). Both of these values are above average and close to 
100 percent, which shows that the model effectively 
diagnoses such categories. This is important when 
determining how suitable the model of the desired 
separation of data kinds in a ransomware attack.

comparison of ransomware attacks. The x-axis lists the

number ranging between 0 percent and 100 percent.

represented in orange color. Precision and recall show 
almost identical trends, both close to 100% in all categories, 
which suggests that the given detection method works well 
for every type of ransomware and normal activity.

Computational results of RANSOMNET+, EDVT-MSSAN,

reduces the error rate but also offers functionality with 
significantly improved speed compared to other methods. 
V. CONCLUSION 
The proposed HQCCNN-BSTO approach proves to 
be very efficient and precise in the context of ransomware 
attack detection. By utilizing QCNN, added with BSTO, the 
proposed model demonstrates deterministic performance 
with an accuracy of 99.7% and an F1 score of 99.5%, which 
is far better than the existing techniques. The work also 
incorporates an enhanced preprocessing step employing 
correlation coefficients followed by min-max normalization, 
and the precise feature selection is based on the Hybrid 
Osprey Optimization Algorithm and the Enhancing Sparrow 
Search Algorithm to guarantee data robustness and feature 
relevancy. Furthermore, the proposed method includes an 
error rate of only 0.01%, while the computation time is very 
brief, 0.02 seconds. It also suggests that it can be used in 
real-time and also for the massive scope of cybersecurity 
applications. Overall, thus, the HQCCNN-BSTO method 
proves to be efficient in countering the new pattern of 
ransomware threats and provides a feasible and efficient 
solution for protecting the systems and data from severe 
ransomware attacks.

TITLE:   Neural networks for estimating Macro Asset Pricing model in football clubs
AUTHORS: ['David Alaminos', 'Ignacio Esteban', 'M. Belén Salas']
YEAR:    2023   VENUE: Intelligent Systems in Accounting, Finance and Management
DOI:     10.1002/isaf.1532   ARXIV: None
TAGS:    ['quantum-ml-finance']
-- ABSTRACT --
This empirical study applies non-linear neural network methods (Deep Recurrent Convolutional Neural Networks, Deep Neural Decision Trees, and Quantum Neural Networks) to estimate a Macro Asset Pricing model for listed European football clubs. The authors report high predictive accuracy (over 90% for top methods), find the model residual accounts for roughly 40% of the variance in the price-dividend ratio, and highlight the importance of long-term risks and habit formation, with the Quantum Neural Network giving the best performance.
-- METHOD --
The authors estimate a Macro Asset Pricing (MAP) model for listed football clubs by decomposing the log price-dividend ratio into four state components (long-run growth xt, long-run volatility ~σ2_t, surplus consumption/habit ~s_t, and a permanent residual e_t). They implement three non-linear estimation methods: a Deep Recurrent Convolutional Neural Network (DRCNN), Deep Neural Decision Trees (DNDT), and a hybrid Quantum Neural Network (QNN) (quantum-inspired neural architecture). Monthly consumption, dividend and share-price data (Eurostat consumption; dividends and prices from STOXX Europe Football index and Bureau van Dijk Amadeus) for the period 2011–2020 are used. Priors for model para
