machine learning and deep learning are being used to protect users from economic and reputational damage: Existing limitations While applicable in certain situations, current phishing detection techniques have a number of significant drawbacks The accuracy of phishing detection is increased by hybrid models such as DNN-LSTM, which combine character-based connections with high-level NLP characteristics. However, they are not appropriate for real-time applications due to their computational complexity. Similar to this, CNN-based methods have limited scalability since they rely on particular kernel variants, which limits their capacity to adapt to a variety : of datasets despite being lightweight and successful in feature selection. Although they have domain-: specific advantages, novel approaches like the binary bat algorithm and LBPS with BP neural networks struggle with generalizability and broader applicability. Moreover, hybrid feature-based techniques such as PDHF include delays that are inappropriate for time-sensitive situations while optimizing accuracy in the order of computing overhead_
Overcoming limitations with the proposed method The novel two-stage methodology of the proposed OptSHQCNN architecture successfully addresses these issues. The robustness of feature selection is increased during the pre- deployment phase by thorough preprocessing, which removes invalid and unnecessary features, and the CBAM, which isolates crucial properties. The complexity seen in hybrid models is reduced using the RKOA, which guarantees a computationally effective selection of essential features In order to improve scalability and applicability across a variety of datasets, OptBERT'$ integration for URL encoding after deployment guarantees that the model preserves significant contextual information: By further optimizing classification and lowering computational 
-- RESULTS WINDOW 4 [86475:89175] --
ion, we might need to enhance feature engineering and speed up the training process This would enable us to confirm the website' s current status and increase the correctness of the training process.
CONCLUSION AND FUTURE SCOPE In order to tackle the crucial problem of phishing detection, the proposed investigation presents OptSHQCNN, an effective and novel two-stage deep learning architecture. In order to ensure thorough data handling and feature analysis, the methodology is divided into pre-deployment and post-deployment phases. Data imbalances, irrelevant features; and missing values are efficiently managed by pre-deployment preprocessing and the convolutional block attention module (CBAM) precisely improves feature extraction. The red kite optimization algorithm (RKOA) increases model efficiency by making sure that only essential features are chosen. By fine- tuning hyperparameters with the shuffled shepherd optimization algorithm (SSOA), the shallow hybrid quantum-classical convolutional neural network (SHQCNN) improves classification accuracy even more Post-deployment URL encoding using Optimized Bidirectional Encoder Representations from Transformers (OptBERT) enhances feature representation and classification by capturing the complex patterns of phishing URLs
37/40

The results, which showed over 99.72% accuracy, 99.76% precision, 99.32% recall, 0.34% FPR and 99.54% Fl-score; demonstrate the effectiveness that the proposed structure performs in comparison to current techniques Numerous benefits arise from the models capacity to incorporate advanced deep learning and optimization methodologies It provides great dependability by reducing false positives and negatives  Scalability and adaptation to various datasets and phishing scenarios are made feasible by the modular two- -stage architecture. OptBERT's contextual encoding ensures improved identification of intricate phishing URLs, while quantum-inspired SHQCNN speeds up computations without impacting accuracy. These additions enable the framework to be used in real-world applications like browser plugins and instant messaging app integrations, promoting safer online spaces and serving as a standard for phishing detection research in the future While this study' emphasizes URL-based feature extraction due to its suitability for real-time detection, it acknowledges the limited consideration of other informative aspects, such as the visual similarity of web content and user interaction behaviour: Incorporating these features in future work may further enhance the model $ performance in complex and deceptive phishing scenarios.

TITLE:   A Review of Routing and Resource Optimization in Quantum Networks
AUTHORS: ['Md. Shazzad Hossain Shaon', 'Mst Shapna Akter']
YEAR:    2026   VENUE: MDPI (open access review article)
DOI:        ARXIV: None
TAGS:    ['cryptography-security']
-- ABSTRACT --
This review surveys recent advances in entanglement routing, quantum key distribution (QKD), and qubit/resource management for short- and long-distance quantum communication, focusing on optimization techniques such as integer programming, reinforcement learning and collaborative heuristics. It evaluates methods by throughput, scalability and fairness, highlights challenges including decoherence, limited resources, dynamic adaptation and error correction, and advocates for hybrid quantum–classical designs and integration with existing infrastructure to enable practical, secure quantum networks.
-- METHOD --
This paper is a scoping literature review conducted following PRISMA-ScR guidelines. The authors performed literature identification, duplicate removal, abstract-level screening and full-text inclusion to arrive at a final set of 64 studies (see their Figure 2). Rather than primary experiments, the review organizes prior work into a taxonomy by methodological focus (RL/DRL-driven adaptive routing, resource-aware optimization and multi-objective routing, fidelity/noise robustness and QKD methods, and scalability/deployment strategies). For comparative analysis the authors extracted and compared studies using common criteria: target objectives (fidelity, throughput, request success rate, laten

A Review of Routing and Resource Optimization in

Md. Shazzad Hossain Shaon

Abstract
Quantum computing is a new discipline that uses the ideas of quantum physics to do cal-
culations that are not possible with conventional computers. Quantum bits, called qubits,
could exist in superposition states, making them suitable for parallel processing in contrast
to traditional bits. When it comes to addressing complex challenges like proof simulation,
optimization, and cryptography, quantum entanglement and quantum interference provide
exponential improvements. This survey focuses on recent advances in entanglement rout-
ing, quantum key distributio
-- RESULTS WINDOW 1 [13590:20603] --
pproaches, including
collaborative optimization and multi-path distribution strategies, similarly highlight the
need to manage constrained entanglement and repeater resources while maintaining stable
performance [27,29]. This line of work complements RL/DRL methods by making trade-
offs explicit, but it also underscores those improvements in that one objective can degrade
another, reinforcing the need for mechanisms that can balance multiple constraints while
remaining responsive to changing conditions.


5 of 25
(3) Fidelity, noise robustness, and secure communication.
Another major research direction focuses on robustness under noise and decoherence
to preserve usable entanglement fidelity and enable secure communication. Shubha et al.
showed that robust long-distance entanglement distribution can be supported by reducing
noise-induced fidelity loss through encoded Bell pairs and generalized circuits [33]. Islam
et al. validated single-qubit QKD methods under realistic noise settings, demonstrating
feasibility for secure communication beyond idealized assumptions [34]. As outlined in
Figure 1, these studies shift attention toward physical-layer reliability and security, while
also suggesting that fidelity-preserving techniques may introduce additional overhead that
must be reconciled with network-layer efficiency goals.
(4) Scalability and deployment-oriented strategies.
Scaling quantum networks requires both algorithmic advances and infrastructure-
aware planning. Heuristic methods for optimal repeater placement have been explored to
support low-cost, long-distance quantum communication, offering practical deployment
guidance with lower computational complexity [35]. At the same time, DRL-based routing
and distributed optimization approaches indicate scalable control mechanisms that can
better adapt to evolving network states [30,31]. As shown in Figure 1, these strategies rep-
resent complementary paths toward real-world scalability, highlighting a tension between
the simplicity and deployability of heuristics and the adaptivity of learning-based control
as networks grow in size and variability.
Across these themes, RL/DRL approaches [26,28,30,31,35] have become central for
adapting to stochastic links and dynamic configurations, while multi-objective and resource-
aware methods [27,29,32] emphasize explicit trade-offs under constrained resources.
Fidelity- and noise-robust techniques [33,34] highlight the increasing importance of real-
istic physical-layer constraints for dependable and secure operation. As synthesized in
Figure 1, the literature collectively motivates integrated approaches that can jointly balance
adaptivity, resource efficiency, fidelity robustness, and scalability for practical quantum
network implementations.
To position our review within the broader literature, we compare it with representative
existing surveys and overview papers on quantum networking. Table 1 summarizes
the scope and focus of these works and highlights what they cover well and what they
leave open. This comparison clarifies the need for the present review and motivates
our emphasis on a thematic organization and cross-study synthesis across routing, QKD-
oriented methods, and deployment-oriented perspectives.

PRISMA-ScR Reporting Statement: This scoping review was conducted following the
Preferred Reporting Items for Systematic Reviews and Meta-Analyses extension for Scoping
Reviews (PRISMA-ScR) guidelines to ensure transparent and comprehensive reporting of
the methodology. The literature selection and screening process, including the identification
of studies, exclusion of duplicates, abstract-level screening, and final inclusion of 64 studies,
is summarized in Figu
-- RESULTS WINDOW 2 [25310:28010] --
 (G = (V, E)). This structure consists of
teleportation nodes and an embedded system (ES), which stipulates communication and
processing. The configurations of the experimental setup are presented in Table 1, and the
key parameters (memory, initial fidelity, and EPR success probability) are varied across
four configurations.
According to the study, the proposed qRL technique outperforms all baseline ap-
proaches, with around 20% greater fidelity in Config1, which models high-resource net-


9 of 25
works. Notably, in Config4, which correlates to low-resource situations, qRL maintains a
fidelity of greater than 0.6, whereas the qDijkstra technique falls below 0.4. In bigger net-
works with n > 30, redundant routes improve system stability and dependability. Moreover,
qRL accurately maintains a request success rate of more than 80% under a 3000-request
demand, even in the resource-constrained Config4 environment. Traditional approaches,
such as R0 and qDijkstra, show severe performance deterioration, with success rates falling
below 50% under intense network loads. This demonstrates the stability and scalability of
the qRL framework in dynamic network situations.
Comparative takeaway: Compared with heuristic baselines (qDijkstra and rule-based
routing), qRL improves adaptivity under changing resource and load conditions, which
helps sustain both fidelity and request success in constrained regimes. The trade-off is
additional learning and tuning overhead (e.g., reward design and exploration strategy),
and performance generalization depends on the reported simulation assumptions.
In another study, the authors propose proactive entanglement swapping and entan-
glement caching to improve quantum routing algorithms [28], particularly SEER [44] and
REPS [45]. The technique successfully stores idle entangled connections for future use,
while applying RL to strategically swap entanglements in high-demand network segments.
The workflow of the proposed approach in [28] is shown in Figure 5.
Figure 5. The overall systematic procedure of Deep Q-Learning (DQRL) study based on their instructions.
The study analyzes and optimizes the quantum routing process using several mathe-
matical formulations, such as the Bellman equation, swapped segment, topology matrix,
request matrix, and segment vector. All the equations can be stated as follows:

The proposed DQRL technique improves request success rates for both SEER and
REPS. In the instance of SEER, DQRL raises the success rate by 48%, from 50% to 74%.
Similarly, REPS’ success rate improves by 61%, jumping from 45% to 73%. Additionally,
the duration of entanglement has a significant impact on the effectiveness of entanglement
caching. When entangled links endure actively for six or more time slots, caching improves
request success rates by 12% to 19%. Furthermore, the chance of effective entanglement
switching is critical for network performance. When the swap probability is increased to
0.9, the system achieves a 55% improvement over the baseline, highlighting the importance
of effective entanglement swapping in improving quantum network stability.
Comparative takeaway: Compared with baseline SEER/REPS and heuristic proactive
swapping, this RL-guided caching and swapping strategy provides higher request suc-
cess rates by explicitly targeting high-demand segments and reusing idle entanglement
resources. The approach is particularly effective when entanglement lifetime and swap
success probability are sufficiently high, suggesting a trade-off in which gains depend on
underlying physical-layer reliability and assumed timing parameters.

served within each time window. DRL is primarily used for dynamic request scheduling
to improve resource allocation across qubits and repeaters. The authors also argue that
the approach supports scalability by keeping training and routing complexity polynomial

The assessment findings show that both DQN and DRN have high success rates in
qubit-rich situations, ranging from 80% to 90%. Even under severe settings when c_i = 2,


12 of 25
they retain a success rate of 60% to 75%, exceeding baseline approaches by 20% to 40%. In

earlier. Additionally, the SNN scales quadratically with network size, indicating an increase
in parameter count as network complexity rises.
Comparative takeaway: Relative to heuristic baselines that rely on fixed shortest-

prioritizes qubit availability in routing decisions. This improves success rate under both
qubit-rich and -constrained regimes, but introduces additional training overhead and a
growing parameter footprint as network size increases.

In convoluted networks with 16 nodes and white noise, PPO beats Monte Carlo by

13 of 25
Carlo. PPO responds to reversible phase shifts, lowering the number of steps required to
39,000 compared to Monte Carlo’s 60 million. In dynamic network situations, PPO retains
93.4% functionality, considerably outperforming Monte Carlo’s 33.1% under changing
error scenarios.
Comparative takeaway: Compared with classical shortest-path routing (Dijkstra) and
sampling-heavy optimization (Monte Carlo), PPO introduces adaptive decision-making
that explicitly accounts for quantum noise models, which improves robustness in ampli-
tude damping and phase-noise settings. The main limitation is the reliance on extensive
training interactions and the dependence of performance on the assumed noise model and
topology configuration
In another study, Le et al. [29] propose a deep reinforcement learning approach for
entanglement routing in quantum networks and introduce the Deep Quantum Routing
Agent (DQRA), a learning-based routing system designed to improve network performance
by increasing the number of successfully served communication requests within a given
time window. The method combines a deep neural network for request scheduling with
a qubit-preserving shortest-path strategy for route selection. The quantum network is
modeled as a graph G = (V, E), where V denotes quantum nodes and E denotes entangled
links. The routing objective is formulated to maximize the effective routing rate by selecting
efficient paths for source–destination pairs under resource constraints. The DQRA frame-
work represents the environment state using network topology, available qubit capacities,
and pending requests. Actions correspond to scheduling and routing decisions for entangle-
ment requests, while the reward function is designed to encourage serving more requests
and penaliz

to improve network performance, greatly increasing predicted throughput and service
rates, especially in high-concurrency settings, while ensuring fairness and effective resource

However, the study proved that COSP showed a 50% increase in average predicted
throughput over earlier methods, particularly in high concurrency conditions. COSP also

Comparative takeaway: Compared with RL-based routing methods, COSP relies
on explicit path search and resource estimation, which can provide more interpretable
decision logic and avoid training overhead. However, the approach may incur higher
online computation due to multi-stage optimization and MCTS, and performance depends
on the assumed topology model and probabilistic resource estimates.
In another study [35], the authors address the challenge of deploying quantum re-
peaters in large-scale quantum networks at reasonable cost. Quantum repeaters are required
to extend communication distance by mitigating fidelity degradation during transmission.
To reduce the number of repeaters while preserving end-to-end connectivity, the study
proposes two heuristic placement strategies, the Multi-Center Approach (MCA) and the
Single-Center Approach (SCA). These heuristics aim to obtain near-optimal solutions sub-
stantially faster than Integer Linear Programming (ILP), which can become computationally
expensive for large networks [50]. The experimental settings and evaluation metrics are
summarized in Table 6.
MCA operates in two phases: center selection and center connection. In center selec-
tion, nodes are chosen as candidate repeater locations based on connectivity and coverage,
with additional centers added when the distance between leaf-node pairs exceeds the
maximum transmission range Lmax. The process continues until the network is covered
by the selected centers. In the center connection phase, inter-center links are established,
and intermediate nodes are introduced when distances exceed Lmax. The study further
explores connection strategies, including an MST-based method that adds repeaters where
needed and a more exhaustive variant that considers additional nodes to improve connec-
tivity [50–52]. Figure 8 illustrates the overall methodology.

The reported results indicate that the heuristic methods substantially reduce computa-
tion time compared with ILP. For example, the study notes that ILP can take days to solve
a network with 54 nodes, whereas SCA can produce a feasible solution within seconds,
illustrating the practical advantage of heuristic placement in large-scale settings.


16 of 25
Comparative takeaway: Compared with RL-based routing methods that optimize
online decisions, repeater-placement heuristics target the infrastructure layer by reducing
required hardware while keeping connectivity constraints. The main benefit is scalability
and fast runtime relative to ILP, while the limitation is that solution quality depends on
heuristic design choices and network topology assumptions.
3.3. Entanglement and QKD Based Methods
Recent quantum networking studies increasingly rely on entanglement-routing opti-
mization and QKD-oriented protocol design to improve reliability, throughput, and security
under realistic constraints [32–34]. This subsection summarizes how these studies structure
their methods and what their findings imply for future deployment.
In study [32], Yiming et al. propose a two-stage framework for entanglement routing
that aims to maximize both the number of served quantum-user pairs and the expected
throughput. The offline stage focuses on selecting a set of quantum-user pairs and their
primary routes by solving an ILP formulation. Because the offline problem is NP-complete,
the authors relax binary decision variables to continuous values, solve the resulting linear
program, and recover an integer solution using branch-and-bound. Candidate paths are
generated using shortest-path-based selection (via Yen’s method) to limit resource usage
and form a set of primary routing paths.
In the online stage, the objective shifts to max

approximation ratio of 2, resulting in efficient
resource use and high-performance quantum communication [54]. Figure 9 shows the
overall solving problems of the study for three stages. Additionally, Table 7 summarizes
their setups.

The authors compare their proposed algorithms, MULTI-R and MR-REC, to benchmark
methods, emphasizing important findings. MULTI-R reliably serves all 20 quantum-user
pairs, exceeding FER, Q-PASS [55,56], and B1; however, FER suffers in resource-constrained
networks. MR-REC achieves the highest predicted throughput, improving by up to 55%
above FER, whereas MULTI-R falls behind but still outperforms FER. Despite having
the maximum throughput, ALG-4 loses fairness by providing fewer pairings. In terms of
resilience, when 10% of the switches go offline, MR-REC has a greater decline in throughput
but stays stable, with losses remaining less than twice the ratio of offline switches.
Comparative takeaway: Compared with greedy and heuristic baselines (FER, Q-
PASS, and hop-based routing), the proposed MULTI-R/MR-REC framework provides
more systematic control over the trade-off between fairness (serving more user pairs) and
throughput (expected ebits per unit time) by jointly optimizing path selection and qubit
allocation. Unlike approaches that only optimize routing paths, it explicitly includes a
recovery mechanism to handle link failures, improving resilience. However, relative to
learning-based routing methods, it relies on ILP and branch-and-bound procedures, which
introduce higher computational overhead and make scalability and runtime more sensitive
to network size and optimization constraints.


18 of 25
In another study, Ref. [34], proposed a multi-qubit GHZ state-based QKD system that
employs single qubit transmission and QND measurements to transmit multiple classical
bits. The suggested QKD paradigm involves Alice generating L+1 GHZ state qubits and
sending one to Bob. She generates L auxiliary qubits and encodes key values (1 as |1〉|1〉
and 0 as |0〉|0〉). Alice does a Bell State Measurement (BSM) between the first entangled
qubit and the first auxiliary qubit and reports the result to Bob. Bob applies suitable
gates depending on the BSM results and tests his qubit using the QND measurement.

Comparative takeaway: Compared with standard single-qubit QKD protocols, the
GHZ-based design improves protocol efficiency by enabling multiple key bits per transmit-
ted qubit via multi-qubit entanglement and QND measurement. However, it requires more
complex state preparation and control operations, and its practical benefit depends on the
feasibility of maintaining GHZ entanglement and performing reliable measurements under
realistic noise and resource constraints.
In another study [33], the authors wanted to improve fidelity for encoded quantum bell
pairs for long and short-distance communication, together with the generalized network
illustrated in Figure 10. A flowchart describing the major phases in the process might help
visualize the technique. To begin, a Bell state |φ +〉|ϕ +〉is created using Hadamard and
CNOT gates.
 
Figure 10. Overall procedure and methodology of the study [33].
The Bell state is encoded using a Quantum Repetition Code (QRC) with 2k + 1 qubits.
The ancilla qubits are initialized to |0〉and entangled with the main qubits via CNOT gates.
The encoded qubits are sent over a quantum channel, resulting in bit flip or phase flip
errors. Short-distance communication uses stabilizer formalism to assess symptoms and
repair faults locally. To measure symptoms across great distances, Alice and Bob employ
a traditional communication channel, which allows them to communicate measurement
findings and fix mistakes globally. Finally, the fidelity of the Bell state is determined
following error correction to assess the protocol’s performance. The entire scheme was
simulated using the IBM Qiskit QASM simulator.
Comparative takeaway: Compared with routing-centric or scheduling-centric meth-
ods, this work focuses on physical-layer robustness by improving entanglement fidelity
through encoding and error correction. The approach can enhance reliability under noise,
but it introduces additional qubit overhead (2k + 1 encoding) and operational complexity,
which may increase resource demands when integrated into larger network-level protocols.
3.4. Cross-Study Trends and Comparative Synthesis
Across the reviewed studies, a clear trend is the transition from static heuristic or
shortest-path routing toward adaptive RL/DRL-based control, mainly to handle stochastic


20 of 25
entanglement generation and dynamic network conditions. In parallel, optimization
and heuristic approaches increasingly adopt multi-objective formulations that balance
fidelity, latency, and resource utilization, reflecting the practical scarcity of qubits, memories,
and entanglement resources. At the physical layer, fidelity- and QKD-oriented designs
place stronger emphasis on robustness under noise and decoherence, but often introduce
additional operational overhead that must be reconciled with end-to-end throughput and
request success. When comparing scalability, learning-based control can generalize to
changing conditions but depends on state design, training cost, and simulator realism,
whereas optimization-based methods provide structured decision-making but may become
computationally expensive as network size grows. Overall, the literature indicates a
convergence toward deployment-aware, cross-layer designs that jointly consider physical-
layer noise, resource-aware scheduling, and adaptive routing; however, reproducible
benchmarking remains challenging due to inconsistent assumptions, metrics, and reporting
practices across studies. These observations motivate the consolidated challenges and
future opportunities discussed in the next section.
4. Challenges & Future Scope
Despite rapid progress, practical quantum communication still faces a set of technical
and engineering barriers that its limit large-scale deployment.
The first major challenge is end-to-end scalability under loss and decoherence. As
distance increases, photon loss in fiber or free-space links and time-de
-- RESULTS WINDOW 7 [64287:66987] --
prioritize end-to-end
system engineering, including atmospheric effects, link intermittency, integration with
terrestrial backbone routing, and consistent security and performance evaluation [59,63].
5. Conclusions
Quantum networking holds great promise for secure communication and distributed
computing, but resource limitations remain a major challenge. Constraints in qubit capacity,
entangled-pair storage, and coherence time hinder both reliability and scalability in practi-
cal systems. Additionally, high energy demands, hardware inefficiencies, and the fragility
of quantum states make large-scale deployment difficult outside of controlled laboratory
environments. Network-level issues, such as routing entanglement over long distances,
synchronizing quantum operations between distant nodes, and mitigating noise, further
complicate real-world implementation. Advancements in quantum memory, fault-tolerant
error correction, photonic interfaces, and hybrid quantum–classical integration are essen-
tial for overcoming these barriers and achieving robust, scalable quantum networks in
the future.

-- ABSTRACT --
This chapter reviews how AI, blockchain, quantum computing, IoT and cloud technologies are reshaping financial services by improving risk assessment, fraud detection, customer experience and operational efficiency. It surveys applications, benefits and key challenges (security, scalability, privacy, ethics and regulation) and outlines future directions including post-quantum cryptography, federated learning and AI-driven financial modeling. This chapter examines the convergence of artificial intelligence and quantum computing for financial modeling and risk assessment, arguing that AI improves predictive analytics while quantum algorithms can exponentially accelerate optimization and simulat
-- METHOD --
This chapter is primarily a narrative review synthesizing literature on the impact of emerging technologies (AI, blockchain, quantum computing, IoT, cloud) on financial services and assessing their benefits, risks, and regulatory/ethical implications. In addition to the literature synthesis, the authors include a focused comparative case-study-style quantitative analysis (mixed-methods approach) to illustrate the real-world impact of emerging technologies: data were collected from five leading banks and fintech firms that adopted RPA, AI and blockchain/cloud automation during 2020–2023. Case Study: Impact of Emerging Technologies on Financial Services Efﬁciency 
The ﬁnancial services industry has undergone a massive transformation with the 
adoption of emerging technologies such as Artiﬁcial Intelligence (AI), Blockchain, 
Quantum Computing, Internet of Things (IoT), and Cloud Computing. This case 
study analyzes the quantitative impact of these technologies on ﬁnancial institutions, 
focusing on efﬁciency, cost reduction, risk management, and customer satisfaction. 
The study is based on data collected from global banks, ﬁntech ﬁrms, and ﬁnancial 
service providers that have integrated these technologies into their operations. 
Objective 
The primary objective of this case study is to evaluate the impact of emerging 
technologies on ﬁnancial institutions using key performance indicators (KPIs) such 
as: 
Reduction in operational costs. 
Increase in transaction speed and efﬁciency. 
Fraud detection accuracy improvement. 
Customer satisfaction ratings before and after implementation. 
Data Collection and Methodology 
The study collected data from ﬁve leading banks and ﬁntech companies that 
adopted AI, blockchain, and cloud-based automation over a period of three years 
(2020–2023). The data was gathered from internal reports, ﬁnancial statements, and 
customer surveys. 
The quantitative results were analyzed using statistical comparison models, and 
performance improvements were measured against industry benchmarks.

3 
AI Techniques for Synthetic Data Generation 
Artiﬁcial intelligence (AI) has revolutionized the ﬁeld of synthetic data generation 
by enabling the creation of high-quality, realistic ﬁnancial datasets. Various AI tech-
niques, including deep learning and probabilistic models, allow ﬁnancial institutions 
to generate data that closely mimics real-world patterns while preserving privacy 
and security. These AI-driven methods ensure that synthetic ﬁnancial data is both 
statistically accurate and compliant with regulatory requirements. The following 
subsections explore some of the most advanced AI techniques used in synthetic data 
generation for ﬁnance.


39
3.1 
Generative Adversarial Networks (GANs) 
Generative Adversarial Networks (GANs) are one of the most powerful AI techniques 
for generating synthetic ﬁnancial data. Developed by Ian Goodfellow and his team 
in 2014, GANs consist of two competing neural networks: 
Generator: This network creates synthetic data samples that resemble real ﬁnancial 
data, such as stock market transactions, customer proﬁles, or fraud patterns. 
Discriminator: This network evaluates the generated data and distinguishes 
between real and synthetic samples. 
These two networks work in an adversarial manner, meaning that as the generator 
improves its ability to create realistic data, the discriminator becomes better at identi-
fying synthetic samples. Over time, the generator produces highly realistic ﬁnancial 
datasets that can be used for: 
Market trend simulations to predict investment opportunities. 
Fraud detection training by generating synthetic fraudulent transactions. 
Risk modeling for banking and insurance sectors. 
One of the biggest advantages of GANs is their ability to generate highly realistic, 
diverse datasets that preserve complex ﬁnancial relationships, making them ideal for 
training AI models in banking, trading, and credit risk assessment. 
3.2 
Variational Autoencoders (VAEs) 
Variational Autoencoders (VAEs) are another deep learning technique used for 
synthetic data generation in
-- RESULTS WINDOW 4 [152110:158662] --
vent synthetic identity 
fraud. 
Ensuring robust cybersecurity measures will help ﬁnancial institutions harness the 
power of synthetic data while safeguarding ﬁnancial integrity and consumer trust. 
Case Study: AI-Generated Synthetic Data for Credit Risk Assessment 
5.5 
Introduction 
Credit risk assessment is a critical function in the ﬁnancial industry, determining the 
likelihood of a borrower defaulting on a loan. Traditional models rely on historical 
ﬁnancial data, but these datasets often suffer from bias, privacy concerns, and limited 
diversity in borrower proﬁles. AI-driven synthetic data generation offers a promising 
solution by creating realistic yet privacy-compliant ﬁnancial datasets. 
This case study evaluates the effectiveness of AI-generated synthetic data in 
improving credit risk assessment models. We compare synthetic data-enhanced 
models with traditional models based on prediction accuracy, fairness metrics, and 
model robustness. 
5.6 
Methodology 
5.6.1
Data Sources 
Real Dataset: 100,000 loan records from a ﬁnancial institution, including borrower 
details, credit scores, income levels, and loan repayment history. 
Synthetic Dataset: 100,000 AI-generated synthetic records using Generative 
Adversarial Networks (GANs) and Variational Autoencoders (VAEs) trained on 
the real dataset.


47

0.86 
5.6.2
Evaluation Metrics 
The credit risk models were evaluated on: 
Prediction Accuracy (AUC-ROC Score): Measures the model’s ability to distin-
guish between defaulters and non-defaulters. 
Fairness Metrics (Disparate Impact, Equal Opportunity Difference): Assesses bias 
in credit risk predictions across demographic groups. 
Model Robustness (Sensitivity to Data Imbalance): Measures how well models 
handle underrepresented borrower groups. 
5.6.3
Model Training 
Baseline Model: Logistic Regression trained on real data. 
AI-Enhanced Model: Random Forest trained on a combination of real and 
synthetic data. 
5.7 
Results and Discussion 
5.7.1
Prediction Accuracy Improvement 
The model trained with synthetic data achieved a higher AUC-ROC score, indicating 
improved predictive power in identifying high-risk borrowers as shown in Table 2. 
Key Takeaway: The synthetic data-enhanced model improved credit risk predic-
tion by 10.2%, demonstrating that AI-generated datasets enhance ﬁnancial modeling. 
5.7.2
Fairness Analysis 
To ensure fairness, we measured disparate impact (DI) and equal opportunity 
difference (EOD) for gender and race-based groups as shown in Table 3.
The synthetic data-enhanced model reduced bias signiﬁcantly, leading to fairer 
credit decisions.

5.7.3
Robustness to Data Imbalance 
The real dataset contained only 5% high-risk borrowers, leading to model biases. 
We tested how well each model performed on underrepresented groups. 
The AI-augmented model correctly identiﬁed 50% more high-risk borrowers, 
improving risk management for lenders as shown in Table 4. 
This case study demonstrates that AI-generated synthetic data enhances credit 
risk assessment models by: 
1. Increasing prediction accuracy by 10.2%, leading to better risk management. 
2. Reducing bias in lending decisions, ensuring fairer ﬁnancial opportunities. 
3. Improving model robustness, especially for underrepresented borrower groups. 
By leveraging synthetic data, ﬁnancial institutions can enhance AI-driven 
decision-making while maintaining privacy, compliance, and fairness in lending. 
5.8 
Conclusion 
The integration of AI-driven synthetic data generation in the ﬁnancial industry 
has demonstrated signiﬁcant potential in enhancing data-driven decision-making, 
improving model accuracy, and addressing privacy concerns. This chapter explored 
the role of synthetic data in ﬁnance, highlighting its beneﬁts, challenges, and practical 
applications. 
Through a detailed case study on credit risk assessment, we established that 
synthetic data can improve predictive accuracy by over 10%, reduce bias in ﬁnancial 
models, and enhance robustness to data imbalances. These ﬁndings emphasize the 
value of AI-powered data augmentation in mitigating ﬁnancial risks and ensuring 
fairness in lending practices.


49
However, despite these advantages, challenges such as data ﬁdelity, regulatory 
compliance, and security risks remain critical. Financial institutions must adopt 
rigorous validation frameworks, fairness-aware AI algorithms, and advanced privacy-
preserving techniques to ensure the ethical and responsible use of synthetic data. As 
AI continues to evolve, maintaining a balance between innovation, security, and 
ethical considerations will be essential for the widespread adoption of synthetic data 
in ﬁnance. 
5.9 
Future Work 
While synthetic data generation has proven effective in various ﬁnancial applications, 
several areas require further exploration and development: 
1. Enhancing Realism and Granularity—Future research should focus on devel-
oping more advanced AI models, such as hybrid GAN-VAE architectures, that 
can generate synthetic data with greater granularity and realism, ensuring better 
alignment with real-world ﬁnancial patterns. 
2. Regulatory Compliance and Explainability—There is a growing need to establish 
industry-wide standards and regulatory frameworks for synthetic data usage. 
Future studies should explore explainable AI (XAI) methods to ensure synthetic 
data models are transparent, auditable, and compliant with GDPR, CCPA, and 
ﬁnancial regulations. 
3. Expanding Use Cases in Financial Markets—Beyond credit risk assessment, 
fraud detection, and algorithmic trading, synthetic data can be leveraged for 
portfolio optimization, wealth management, and economic forecasting. Future 
work should investigate how synthetic data can support personalized ﬁnancial 
planning and investment strategies. 
4. Integration with Privacy-Preserving AI Techniques—Combining synthetic data

4.2 
Artiﬁcial Immune System Terminologies 
Currently, we deﬁne the key concepts and terminologies employed in this algorithm 
to simplify the understanding of the AIS functioning. 
bullet Antigen: a feature vector of a signature from the training set, serving as the seed 
for generating synthetic characteristics. 
bullet Antibody: It is a clone generated by mutating a feature vector. 
bullet Afﬁnity: a similarity measure computed between an antigen and antibody. The 
resulted similarity value falls within the range of [0;1]. As such, as lower the 
afﬁnity value is, greater is the similarity between the antigen and antibody. 
bullet The Afﬁnity Threshold (AT): represent the mean afﬁnity value calculated across 
all the antigens of the training set. 
bullet Artiﬁcial Recognition Ball (ARB): It corresponds to a cloned feature vector 
with a resource number and a stimulation value, referring to an antibody or B cell 
within the natural immune system. 
bullet Stimulation: a measure that indicate how an ARB responds to an antigen. A 
higher stimulation value indicates a greater similarity between the ARB and the 
antigen. The stimulation value varies from 0 to 1, it inversely related to afﬁnity. 
bullet Clonal expansion stopping criterion: After the resource competition process, 
the average stimulation is calculated between survived clones and the antigen.


80
N. Arab et al.
If this stimulation exceeds the stimulation threshold the expansion of the set of 
clones is stopped. Otherwise, new clones are generated. 
bullet User-deﬁned parameters: 
– Mutation rate: This is a probability utilized to control the mutation of each 
element in the feature vector. 
– Clonal rate: It t

Experimental Results 
5.1 
Datasets 
The evaluation of the proposed synthetic feature generation scheme is performed 
using two public datasets which are GPDS-300 and MCYT-75. 
GPDS-300: composed of European signatures of 300 writers each with 24 genuine 
signatures and 30 forgeries.


Contribution of Synthetic Features for Improving Signature Veriﬁcation …
85
MCYT-75: contains the Spanish signatures of 75 individuals. Each disposes of 
15 genuine signatures and forgeries. 
5.2 
Evaluation Criteria 
To assess the efﬁciency of the proposed features generation scheme, we employ three 
well-known criteria in the veriﬁcation system which are FAR%, FRR, and AER. 
bullet False Acceptance Rate (FAR): represents the number of imitated signatures 
detected as genuine by the veriﬁcation system. 
bullet False Rejection Rate (FRR): corresponds to the percentage of real signatures 
classiﬁed as imitations by the veriﬁcation system. 
bullet Average Error Rate (AER): corresponds to the mean of FAR and FRR. 
Experiments are carried out according to the standard protocol used in the liter-
ature for performance assessment of the veriﬁcation system. This protocol involves 
using real signatures and random forgeries to perform the training stage, while the 
veriﬁcation stage utilizes real and imitated signatures. More precisely, the training 
stage of the positive class is achieved using several set of features that varies gradually 
from 1 until 5, from which we add a variable number of synthetic features determined 
by the selection criterion. Thus, the negative class of the training contains the same 
number of features as the positive class of random forgeries. The veriﬁcation stage 
is achieved according to two different setups. Regarding the ﬁrst one, all remaining 
genuine and skilled forgeries signatures were used, while in the second one only 10 
genuine and skilled forgeries signatures were employed. Table 1 and 2 summarize 
the results obtained in experiments. 
The outcomes presented in Table 1 indicate that adding synthetic features vectors 
is beneﬁc regardless the conﬁguration. The performance improvement is particularly 
notable when a reduced set of real signatures is available only 1 or 2, in this case gains 
of about 15% and 5% are respectively obtained when the evaluating the veriﬁcation 
system on all remaining signatures. Besides, when testing with 10 genuine and skilled 
signatures, a considerable rise of gains exceeding 17% and 6% achieved using 1

and 2 signatures respectively. Moreover, employing 3 genuine signatures yields an 
enhancement of 3.5% of the error rates. Furthermore, as the number of real signatures 
increases to 4 or 5, the obtained improvement is limited to only 1%. This founding 
can be interpreted by the fact that with such number of signatures the veriﬁcation 
system achieves stability, and adding more samples slightly affect its performance. 
The results obtained from the MCYT-75 exhibit a similar behavior compared to 
the PGDS-300 dataset. Using a single genuine signature, yields a gain of around 
14% when testing with all remaining signatures and 17% when testing with only 
10 genuine and skilled forgeries. Besides, with two genuine signatures, the gain 
improvement is around about 4% regardless the size of the test set. However, when 
using 4 or 5 genuine signatures the gain is limited to 1%. Such outcomes are expected 
since with 5 samples the system is already perform well and adding more samples 
does not affect signiﬁcantly the veriﬁcation performance. 
It is important to note that for both datasets, the dynamic selection criterion selects 
a moderate number of synthetic features vectors was added, around double or triple of 
the number of genuine samples. Additionally, the improvement achieved when testing 
the system with 10 genuine and skilled forgeries signatures surpass that obtained 
when testing with all remaining signatures. In fact, As the number of tested signa-
tures increases, the system encounters greater difﬁculty in accurately classifying the 
questioned signature due to heightened intra-variability. 
6 
Conclusion 
This work deals with the data shortage issue in the signature veriﬁcation system. 
In various applications only a small number of real signatures is available. This 
limitation obstructs the development of a solid SVS. To address this issue, we utilized 
the artiﬁcial immune system to generate synthetic features. AIS algorithm mimics 
principal functions of the natural immune system like the clonal expansion, and 
mutation and meta dynamic process. Moreover, to select only the b synthetic features 
that having exhibiting greater resemblance to the antigen we introduce a dynamic 
threshold that selects only effective synthetic features. The performance assessment 
was conducted using two distinct test sets. The ﬁrst set comprises all remaining real


Contribution of Synthetic Features for Improving Signature Veriﬁcation …
87
and imitated signatures, while the second set contains only 10 real and 10 imitated 
signatures. The enhancement is notably more pronounced when only one or two 
genuine signatures are available, resulting in a 17% gain for both the GPDS-300 
and MCYT-75 datasets. Additionally, with 2 genuine signatures, the improvement 
remains substantial, approximately 6% for the GPDS-300 and 3.5% for the MCYT-75 
dataset. However, in the case of 4 or 5 real samples, the improvement is less notable, 
hovering around 1% for both datasets. Based on these ﬁndings, one can conclude 
that the data augmentation techniques are crucial when a one or two real signatures 
are used. As the number of genuine signatures increase, the system stabilizes and 
adding more synthetic features does not bring a signiﬁcate improvement.

TITLE:   Quantum Machine Learning: Algorithms and Applications in Quantum Computing
AUTHORS: ['Priya Deshmukh', 'Benjamin Carter']
YEAR:    2024   VENUE: International Journal on Advanced Electrical and Computer Engineering
DOI:        ARXIV: None
TAGS:    ['quantum-ml-finance']
-- ABSTRACT --
This review surveys quantum machine learning (QML) techniques that combine quantum computing and classical machine learning, covering quantum data encoding, variational algorithms (e.g., VQE, QAOA), quantum neural networks, quantum SVMs, QBMs and QGANs. It highlights potential quantum speedups for optimization and classification, applications in drug discovery, finance and cryptography, and discusses current hardware limitations, noise challenges, and the role of hybrid quantum-classical approaches.
-- METHOD --
The paper describes a hybrid quantum-classical methodology typical of Variational Quantum Algorithms (VQAs). Classical data are first encoded into quantum states using encoding schemes (e.g., amplitude, basis, and angle encoding) via quantum operators (the paper refers generically to operators V^1, V^2, V^3). Parameterized quantum circuits (unitaries U^(Φ1), U^(Φ2), U^(Φ3) in the text) are then applied to process these quantum-encoded features and extract quantum-enhanced representations. Measurements are performed on the output states and multiple samples are taken to estimate expectation values and mitigate probabilistic measurement noise. Extracted classical observables are fed into class

field that integrates quantum computing with classical machine 
learning techniques to enhance computational efficiency and solve 
complex problems beyond the capabilities of classical systems. This 
paper explores fundamental QML algorithms, including qu

in applications such as quantum-enhanced image

Methodology 
The overall process of a Quantum Machine 
Learning (QML) model follows a hybrid quantum-
classical approach, leveraging both quantum 
computational power and classical optimization 
techniques. The process begins with encoding 
classical data into quantum states using specific 
quantum operators that map input features into a 
quantum Hilbert space. This step is crucial, as it 
allows classical information to be processed in a 
quantum 
framework, 
utilizing 
the 
unique 
properties of quantum mechanics such as 
superposition and entanglement. Various quantum 
encoding techniques, such as amplitude encoding, 
basis encoding, and angle encoding, are used to 
represent data efficiently within a quantum system. 
Once the data is transformed into a quantum 
representation, the next step involves applying 
quantum transformations using parameterized 
quantum circuits. These circuits manipulate 
quantum 
states 
by 
implementing 
unitary 
transformations 
that 
capture 
complex 
data 
patterns and correlations that may not be easily 
accessible 
in 
classical 
systems. 
The 
transformations are executed through a series of 


Quantum Machine Learning: Algorithms and Applications in Quantum Computing 
4 
 
quantum gates, each designed to modify quantum 
information in a way that enhances feature 
extraction, classification, or optimization tasks. 
This step forms the foundation of quantum 
machine 
learning 
models, 
as 
it 
enables 
computational speedups by leveraging quantum 
parallelism. 
Following quantum processing, measurement 
operations are performed to extract classical data 
from the quantum computations. Since quantum 
measurements are probabilistic in nature, multiple 
samples are taken to estimate expectation values, 
ensuring accuracy and reducing inherent quantum 
noise. The extracted classical data is then fed into a 
classical machine learning model, such as a neural 
network or a support vector machine, for further 
analysis and decision-making. At this stage, 
classical 
algorithms 
process 
the 
quantum-
enhanced features, refining patterns, learning 
representations, and making predictions based on 
the available dataset. 
To ensure that the model learns effectively, a cost 
function is computed, which evaluates the model’s 
performance by measuring the difference between 
predicted and actual outcomes. The optimization 
process involves adjusting the parameters of the 
quantum circuit iteratively to minimize the cost 
function. Since quantum computers are not 
inherently 
designed 
for 
gradient-based 
optimization, classical optimization techniques 
such as gradient descent, stochastic gradient 
descent, or more advanced methods like the Adam 
optimizer are employed to update the quantum 
parameters efficiently. These iterative updates 
continue until the model converges to an optimal 
solution, 
achieving 
improved 
accuracy 
and 
efficiency. 
This hybrid quantum-classical workflow is widely 
utilized in Variational Quantum Algorithms (VQAs), 
which form the basis of many modern QML 
applications, including Quantum Neural Networks 
(QNNs), Quantum Support Vector Machines 
(QSVMs), and Quantum Boltzmann Machines 
(QBMs). The integration of quantum and classical 
resources allows for the efficient processing of 
high-dimensional data, potential computational 
speedups, and novel approaches to solving complex 
optimization and pattern recognition problems. As 
quantum hardware continues to advance, this 
approach holds promise for unlocking new 
capabilities in artificial intelligence, data science, 
and computational modeling.

extract classical information.

Conclusion 
Quantum Machine Learning (QML) represents a 
groundbreaking fusion of quantum computing and 
machine learning, offering the potential for 
exponential speedups and enhanced performance 
in complex computational tasks. This paper 
explored various QML algorithms, including 
Quantum Support Vector Machines (QSVMs), 
Quantum Neural Networks (QNNs), Quantum 
Boltzmann Machines (QBMs), Quantum Generative 
Adversarial Networks (QGANs), and Quantum 
Reinforcement Learning (QRL). The comparative 


Quantum Machine Learning: Algorithms and Applications in Quantum Computing 
6 
 
analysis of these algorithms across multiple 
datasets demonstrated their potential advantages 
in 
classification, 
pattern 
recognition, 
and 
optimization tasks. 
Key findings suggest that QML models, particularly 
QNNs 
and 
QSVMs, 
outperform 
classical 
counterparts in high-dimensional data scenarios, 
offering improved accuracy and efficiency. The 
results highlight the promising applications of QML 
in fields such as healthcare, finance, cryptography, 
and artificial intelligence. However, challenges 
remain, including hardware limitations, noise in 
quantum circuits, and efficient data encoding. 
Addressing these issues through advancements in 
quantum error correction, hybrid quantum-
classical frameworks, and scalable quantum 
hardware will be essential for the practical 
deployment of QML algorithms. 
In conclusion, while QML is still in its early stages, 
its rapid development indicates that it has the 
potential to revolutionize machine learning and 
computation. As quantum technologies mature, 
QML is expected to become an integral component 
of next-generation AI systems, unlocking new 
possibilities that are beyond the reach of classical 
computing. 
Further 
research 
into 
quantum 
hardware 
improvements 
and 
algorithmic 
refinements will be crucial in realizing the full 
potential of Quantum Machine Learning.

5 
Discussion and Conclusion 
The EU harmonized AI regulations aim to reduce the risks associated with high-
risk AI systems and develop an ecosystem of trust by proposing a legal framework 
for trustworthy AI [ 8]. With the advent of generative AI, the Final Draft of the EU 
AI Act also focuses on general-purpose AI or AGI as a target of comprehensive 
regulatory framework [ 14]. This paper proposes a framework for organizing an AGI 
system as a legal entity, an LLC and presents vertical and horizontal shields for risks 
and liabilities of AGI businesses. The proposal is expected to mitigate investors’ 
potential risks and promote AI and AGI research and development. In addition, as 
legal entities, AGI LLCs may give a means for constituting an ecosystem of trust. 
This is the answer to the speciﬁc research question, “How do LLCs mitigate poten-
tial risks of investors and enjoy beneﬁts to promote AGI businesses?” The critical 
point of this solution is that an AGI LLC can be established under the current legal 
system without requiring new legislation or legal reform. An AGI business can be 
run under an LLC and provide a traditional vertical liability shield to members and 
managers. Horizontal liability shields can also be implemented by introducing pro-
tected/registered series LLCs. Since the legal situation around protected/registered 
series LLC is premature, more experience is expected to improve the legal framework 
and obtain a deeper understanding of the trust ecosystem. 
The principle of limited liability of members is not only an advantage but also 
a potential risk. If the legal personhood is abused, there may be the “piercing the


Risk Management for Artiﬁcial General Intelligence by Limited …
25
corporate veil” doctrine and the liability of a limited partner to a third party (the 
Companies Act, Article 597) [ 1, p. 223]. 
As for legal personhood, Simon Chesterman pointed out that for practical reasons, 
AI legal personhood could be achieved using existing forms [ 7, p. 843]. This paper 
presented the detailed procedure for establishing legal personhood with the LLC for 
an AGI business. In the future when the experience and cases are accumulated, the 
debates on AI legal personhood are expected to mature.

is supported by The INOUE ENRYO Memorial Grant, TOYO University, and the Hitachi Foundation 
Kurata Incentive (Grant No. 1467).

Evaluation 
5.1 
Experimental Set Up 
The proposed Pod migration technique is evaluated by comparing the Pod’s memory 
usage and the Pod’s CPU usage of the Kubernetes cluster with the Pod migration 
technique used currently in Kubernetes. We organize the Kubernetes cluster with 
one master node and ﬁve worker nodes. We evaluated this Pod migration technique 
with 6 desktops which are quad-core with 8 GB RAM. Persistent volumes can be 
AzureDisk, NFS, RBD (Rados Block Device), CephFS, Cinder (OpenStack volume 
storage), and Glusterfs. 
We now evaluate the performance of our proposed Pod migration technique with 
the presently used Kubernetes-based Pod migration technique. Comparison is done 
in terms of CPU usage and memory usage of the Pod migrated with the proposed 
migration technique with the pod migrated with the current migration technique used 
in Kubernetes, respectively. 
In Fig. 2 the comparison of CPU usage in a nano CPU unit with the usage of a 
Pod with the Kubernetes-based migration technique and after applying the proposed 
Pod migration technique by considering the containers state and redeploying a Pod 
with optimized containers. A new Pod migrated with a Kubernetes-based migration 
technique has high CPU utilization compared to the new Pod redeployed with opti-
mized containers. This concludes that redeploying a Pod with optimized containers 
using the proposed Pod migration technique will provide an efﬁcient Pod migration 
technique in Kubernetes by reducing the usage CPU in nano CPU units. 
Figure 3 the comparison of memory usage of a Pod in bytes with the Kubernetes-
based migration technique after applying the proposed Pod migration technique by 
Fig. 2 Comparison of CPU usage in nano CPU units between Kubernetes-based Pod migration 
and proposed Pod migration


Pod Migration with Optimized Containers Using Persistent …
35
Fig. 3 Comparison of memory usage in bytes between Kubernetes-based Pod migration and pro-
posed Pod migration 
considering the containers’ state and redeploying a new Pod with optimized con-
tainers. A new Pod migrated with a Kubernetes-based migration technique has high 
memory utilization compared to the new Pod redeployed with optimized containers. 
We observed that the cold start time in the proposed Pod migration technique 
is reduced by 50% in CPU usage compared to the present Kubernetes-based Pod 
migration. This concludes that redeploying a Pod with minimal containers using 
the proposed Pod migration technique will provide an efﬁcacious Pod migration 
technique in Kubernetes by contracting memory utilization. The cold start time is 
measured by nano CPU units used to start the Pod with containers. 
This concludes that the proposed Pod migration technique which uses Persistent 
volume to capture the containers state and redeploy uses no additional CPU and 
memory resources than Kubernetes-based migration technique. 
6 
Conclusions and Future Research 
Migrating Pods from one node to another with minimal service disruption is an essen-
tial functionality in Kubernetes. But this is difﬁcult in Kubernetes because the limited 
network capacity between the nodes will impose unacceptably long downtimes and 
slow down migration operations. Moving Pods between nodes would change the life-
cycle of a Pod. ContainerD and CRIU are used as Container Runtime Interface and for 
checkpointing containers in Kubernetes to restore operations in container runtime. 
We proposed a Pod migration technique that captures the state of the containers 
and uses Persistent volumes to store the containers’ state inside a Pod. Using this Pod


36
I. Vasireddy et al.
migration technique Pods in Kubernetes can be migrated with optimized containers. 
We showed that the cold start time and the usage of the resources like CPU and 
memory can be minimized by migrating a Pod with optimized containers. The idea 
is to reduce cold start time and resource usage by migrating pods from one node to 
another, with an optimized containerized Pod, within the Kubernetes cluster.

ake the raw data available from various sources in the government 
information portal and analyze them further to show how the data can be made more 
pertinent and accessible to the stakeholders. 
In Table 1 (mohua.gov.in) data on the ﬁnancial and physical progress under the 
PMAY scheme is given with the numbers speciﬁed for houses at different stages 
of construction. The data obtained for the months of October 2017, January 2018, 
and May 2018, July 2018, September 2018, August 2019, October 2019, December 
2019, February 2020, and November 2020 clearly shows the progress achieved over 
this period since the inception of PMAY scheme and also reveals important aspects 
of progress in incremental terms/month through several quarters.
The data has been partitioned into three category of states, the ﬁrst being the 
data for the 21 major states (these are the states which have received the maximum 
development support in general and where the majority of the population of India 
resides). The second category involves the 8 states that are located in the north eastern 
part of the country and are considered to have been slow in progress and development 
and currently receive much special assistance from the government to ensure speedier 
progress. Under the third category are the 7 union territories which are small in area 
and have governing status different from the major states. 
The ﬁnancial progress is given in terms of the total ﬁnancial investment on houses 
under the PMAY (Urban) scheme, the government assistance involved under the 
PMAY scheme, and the actual funds released as subsidy under the scheme. It is 
this last value that gives a clear indication of the ﬁnancial progress of the PMAY 
scheme. Similarly, the physical progress is presented in terms of the number of 
houses involved under this scheme, the number of houses grounded for construction 
(that is, the construction of these houses have begun), and the number of houses 
completed under the scheme. The last two data give an indication of the progress of 
the PMAY scheme in physical terms. 
It is observed that during the year 2020 total investment and central fund involved 
per month have drastically reduced though there is no big gap in fund released by 
central government with respect to the previous year. It indicates that percentage of 
total fund released out of fund involved is more in 2020 compared to previous year. In 
case of major states, north east states and union territories central assistance released 
is more than central fund involved. This might be due to carry forward unused fund 
is getting added with current released fund. 
In Table 2, which is derived from Table 1, showing incremental physical progress 
per month, it shows the number of houses involved and grounded for construction 
is much lower than in 2020 compared to 2019, though number of houses completed 
are almost same in both periods. It is also observed that number of houses completed 
during 2020 is more than the number of houses grounded for construction. So some 
houses were grounded earlier but completed during 2020.

grounded, and completed from October 2017 to October 2020. Gaps are found

(3) Although number of houses involved under this scheme has remained almost 
the same in major states for the two periods under consideration, it has reduced 
by 53.3% in the north eastern states and by 17.8% in union territories. This 
clearly indicates that north east states and union territories need more attention 
for new approvals of houses under PMAY (U). This trend can be seen in the 
overall country ﬁgures too where the number of houses involved has gone down 
by 2.8%. Although it is expected that these numbers will show a downward 
trend toward the later part of the scheme’s duration, the downward trend seems 
to be happening too early. 
(4) Number of houses grounded for construction has gone down by 7.9% in major 
states, by 17.5% in north east states, by 2.9% in union territories, and by 8.2% 
in the whole country, again indicating a slowdown.

S. B. Ghose
(5) Table 2 shows clearly that actual number of houses grounded for construction 
is much lower than number of houses involved in the major states. There is an 
almost 50% gap in the Period A and 48% gap in Period B. Almost the same trend 
has been observed in north eastern states during Period A (which shows a gap of 
nearly 70%), but this seems to have improved during Period B. The gap could be 
attributed to the delay in release of interest subsidy. The union territories which 
are under the central government seem to be better in managing this gap indi-
cating a faster processing of interest subsidy applications. During period May 
2018–December 2019, total number of houses completed is just more than 50% 
of houses grounded for construction in all states. During pandemic slowdown 
of the PMAY urban scheme and the delay is sanctioning projects which have led 
to low utilization of sanctioned central assistance amount under this scheme. 
Delay in release of subsidy have forced beneﬁciaries to live in incomplete house 
without proper roof. Houses were grounded but not completed. Though more 
than 1 crore houses have been grounded till June 2022 but only around 61 
lakh houses have been completed and delivered under the Pradhan Mantri Awas 
Yojana (Urban). As per PMAY (U) website (May 2022) a budget of Rs 2.01 lakh 
crore has been sanctioned out of which Rs 1.18 lakh crore has been released and 
Rs 1.10 lakh crore has been spent by the center. As per requirement, geo-tagging 
is mandatory as government wants to keep a record of progress of this ﬂagship 
program. Central government (July 2022) has decided not to sanction any new 
house under PMAY (U) and directed state governments to focus on incomplete 
projects. 
5 
Conclusion 
This paper analyzed the data available on the PMAY scheme which aims at providing 
interest subsidy to house owners in order to meet the ambitious goal of “housing for 
all” in India by 2024, over two block periods. To create awareness among stakeholders 
the data portals and the information systems play a very vital role. In this paper, 
we gathered data from PMAY website and portal which and showed how this raw 
data can be converted into more meaningful form, which can be further analyzed 
to produce useful information, thus improving the usability and quality of the data 
provided. It is hoped that this analysis will encourage government policy makers, 
software developers, and decision-makers to create more user-friendly data portals 
to disseminate information more effectively.

faction and intention to reuse it. We hypothesize the following based on the above 
discussion: 
H5a,b,c,d,e: The intention to continue with the mHealth app is positively inﬂu-
enced by AI integration, data access, data storage, interactivity, and playfulness. 
The conceptual model is shown in Fig. 1. 
3 
Research Design 
3.1 
Measures 
The study used scales from existing literature to gauge the variables involved in the 
study. The responses for a total of 52 statements measuring the latent constructs 
were collected on a ﬁve-point Likert scale (1 = strongly disagree to 5 = strongly 
agree). For mHealth self-efﬁcacy, the three-item scale was adapted from Fox and 
Connolly [23]. The scale for perceived vulnerability was adapted from Luo et al. 
[40]. The health consciousness scale was based on Chen [15] and Halvadia et al. 
[29]. The scale for technology anxiety was adopted from Xue et al. [64]. The scale 
for personal innovativeness was based on Bhatt [10]. The scale for app characteristics


mHealth Apps on the Rise: Exploring the Inﬂuence of App …
57
such as data access, data storage, and AI integration was adapted from Kharrazi et al. 
[35] and Gimpel et al. [25]. The interactivity scale was based on Davis et al. [21] 
and the playfulness scale was adapted from Proyer [49] and Kang and Hwang [34], 
respectively. The scales for TAM variables—perceived usefulness, perceived ease of 
use, and intention to continue were adapted from Wu et al. [62] and Bhatt [10]. 
3.2 
Data Collection 
The mHealth apps users were contacted using a convenience sampling method for 
data collection. The questionnaires were administered in physical form and elec-
tronically also. From a total of 550 responses received through the survey, 537 were 
usable in terms of completeness and validity. The sample consists of 43% female 
and 57% male respondents. 32% of respondents were from the age group of 16 to 25 
and 49% were from 26 to 40. 45% of all were salaried and 29% were self-employed. 
48% were graduates and 31% were post-graduates. From the sample, 39% had a 
family monthly income of INR 50 thousand to 1 lakh and 35% had a monthly family 
income of INR thirty thousand to ﬁfty thousand. 
4 
Results 
4.1 
Measurement Model Results 
Before testing the hypothesis, data were analyzed for reliability and validity of the 
scales used for assessing the latent constructs. Conﬁrmatory Factor Analysis was 
performed for this purpose. The results indicated reasonably good model ﬁt (Model 
ﬁt indices values met the cut-off limits prescribed by Hu and Bentler [32]: χ 2/df 
= 2.510, CFI = 0.932, GFI = 0.902, RMSEA = 0.054, RMR = 0.048). Further, 
the factor loading values were > 0.5 for all measured items, and all measured items 
loaded well on their respective constructs. The criteria recommended by Fornell 
and Larcker [22] were applied to appraise whether the scales used for the study are 
reliable and valid. The reliability of the scales was examined based on the Composite 
Reliability (CR) values. The CR values were greater than the minimum cut-off value 
(0.7) required to conﬁrm the reliability [22]. Furthermore, the Average Variance 
Extracted (AVE) values were above the minimum value (0.5) indicating convergent 
validity. Lastly, the square root of the AVE of each construct was compared with 
its correlation with all other constructs to establish the discriminant validity. The 
correlation values are lower than the square root of AVEs. Thus, discriminant validity 
was conﬁrmed.


58
N. Halvadia et al.
4.2 
Hypothesis Testing 
Having checked the scale for validity and reliability, the next step was to test the 
proposed conceptual model. We applied structural equation modeling (SEM) for 
the same. The values of the model ﬁt indices were found acceptable based on the 
prescribed values by Hu and Bentler [32] (χ 2/df = 2.205, CFI = 0.935, GFI = 0.907, 
RMSEA = 0.053, RMR = 0.047). The results of hypothesis testing are shown in 
Table 1. The results supported the hypothesis based on the core TAM model as 
H1, H2, and H3 were accepted. Perceived usefulness (β = 0.670, p < 0.05) and 
perceived ease of use (β = 0.689, p < 0.05) were found to have a signiﬁcant effect on 
users’ intention to continue using the mHealth apps. The results also conﬁrmed the 
relationship between personal characteristics and users’ behavioral intentions. Self-
efﬁcacy was found to have a signiﬁcant effect on behavioral intentions and H4a was 
accepted (β = 0.545, p < 0.05). H4b was also found to be statistically acceptable 
as the effect of health consciousness on intention to continue was signiﬁcant (β 
= 0.572, p < 0.05). The results also evidenced the negative effect of technology 
anxiety on users’ intention (β = − 0.448, p < 0.05) and H4c was supported. H4d 
and H4e were also held up as personal innovativeness (β = 0.393, p < 0.05) and 
vulnerability (β = 0.229, p < 0.05) had a signiﬁcant impact on users’ intention to 
continue using the mHealth apps. Further, the results also conﬁrmed the impact of 
app characteristics on user behavior. Data access and data storage showed a strong 
positive impact on intention to continue and therefore, H5a (β = 0.535, p < 0.05) and 
H5b (β = 0.474, p < 0.05) were conﬁrmed. The effect of interactivity (β = 0.385, 
p < 0.05) and AI integration (β = 0.335, p < 0.05) on behavioral intention were 
afﬁrmed providing evidence for H5c and H5d. Lastly, playfulness was also found 
to signiﬁcantly inﬂuence intention to continue (β = 0.247, p < 0.05) and H5e was 
supported.
5 
Discussion 
The study tested a comprehensive model by incorporating personal characteristics 
and app characteristics into the core TAM model for predicting users’ intention to 
continue usage of mHealth applications. The results supported all the hypothesized 
relationships. The core TAM variables—perceived usefulness and perceived ease 
of use were found to have a signiﬁcant impact on users’ intention to use mHealth 
apps in the future. These ﬁndings were consistent with earlier studies [4, 49]. It 
can be deduced that designing the mHealth app for user-friendliness and ease of 
operation induces users to continue the use of the app. The results also established 
that users with more conﬁdence in their abilities will ﬁnd it less difﬁcult to learn 
the app and its various features and therefore, are more likely to keep using the 
apps. The results indicated that consumers with higher personal innovativeness tend 
to show less resistance to new products and technologies and therefore are more

n been studied in a number of 
publications in the presence of single [7] and multiple [9, 10] untrusted relays. It 
has been found that increasing the number of untrusted relays reduces the secrecy 
performance when compared to trusted relays [9]. 
The performance of a PLS system that employs both CR and CJ schemes can 
be enhanced by optimal power allocation (OPA) methods. The paper [13] presents 
various topics on designs, optimization problems, and performance metrics of PLS. 
The OPA in the presence of single and multiple untrusted relays and potential Eves 
with the DBJ scheme has been studied in [14, 15]. Although DBJ is straightforward 
to implement since the destination can eliminate the jamming signal using the prior 
information of the same, the source-destination direct link in DBJ cannot be used to 
fully take advantage of the beneﬁt of cooperation. The system in [16], employs the 
SBJ technique to achieve positive secrecy. For secrecy maximization, power opti-
mization based on the gradient method is used in the above-mentioned works [8, 
14, 16]. Sometimes, the gradient method used for optimization will not converge to 
produce optimum results, as it has problems with discontinuous, non-differentiable, 
or large dimensionality functions. In our earlier work [5], power optimization by 
the gradient-free Nelder–Mead method (N-M), in untrusted AF cooperative relaying


Study of Power Optimization Algorithms for Secure Communication …
67
scenario employing the SBJ scheme, is presented and its performance is compared 
with the conventional derivative test method. In this paper, a performance compar-
ison of different power optimization algorithms is studied to identify which opti-
mization method is the best for the computation of secrecy in terms of convergence 
as well as complexity. Hence, OPA based on the gradient-free N-M algorithm [17] 
is compared with gradient-based methods—namely, Broyden–Fletcher–Goldfarb– 
Shanno (BFGS) [18] and Conjugate-Gradient (CG) methods [19]. Their performance 
is compared with the two-dimensional exhaustive search algorithm (ES), derivative 
test method (DT), and EPA strategy. The secrecy and complexity of the algorithms 
are studied using MATLAB and R programming simulations. 
The major contributions of the work are outlined as: 
The secrecy performance comparison of a cooperative network that comprises 
multiple untrusted AF relays and an external Eve with three power optimization 
algorithms namely—the Nelder–Mead method, Conjugate-Gradient method, and 
Broyden–Fletcher–Goldfarb–Shanno algorithm, employing SBJ scheme, is formu-
lated. The performance is evaluated for conventional and worst-case untrusted

Table 4 illustrates the complexity of the iterative algorithms, where the average 
number of iterations for different SNR values is tabulated. The table presents the 
results for the symmetric relay position with N = 25, for both the untrusted and 
worst-case scenarios. The complexity of an algorithm is inversely proportional to the 
iterations needed for system convergence, meaning that as complexity increases, so 
does the number of iterations, which increases computing time and memory require-
ments. The table shows that, in both cases, the N-M technique requires more itera-
tions to reach convergence than the other two methods, hence complex. The BFGS 
approach is considered the best option for the symmetric case. The complexity of the 
CG method lies between the N-M and BFGS algorithms. Another thing we can make 
out from the table is that for low SNR, complexity will be higher, and sometimes it is 
difﬁcult for the system to converge. Beyond 10 dB, the number of iterations remains 
almost the same for all the cases. Hence, 10 dB is considered the minimum SNR 
needed for better performance.
Figure 4a, b indicate respectively how the power allocation factors a and as vary 
in accordance with SNR for the symmetric case. It is observed that the N-M method 
allocates more power to the signal than the other two iterative algorithms, i.e., a and 
as are more for the case of N-M. The graphs for BFGS and CG methods overlap as 
their performance in terms of allocation of the signal power is almost the same.
Table 5 presents the results of secrecy performance in the symmetric scenario for 
the different number of relays N. It is clear from the table that the secrecy performance 
degrades as N increases. The results presented here correspond to the conventional 
untrusted scenario. The BFGS iterative method gives the best secrecy performance 
compared with DT, ES, and EPA strategies.

Study of Power Optimization Algorithms for Secure Communication …
77
means of iterative algorithms—N-M, BFGS, and CG methods and their performance 
is compared with the derivative test, exhaustive search methods, and equal power 
allocation strategy. The method that produces maximum secrecy at the expense of 
more signal power allocation is considered the best method. Hence iterative algo-
rithms produce the best results, but there exists a trade-off between the conver-
gence and complexity among them. The N-M gradient-free method converges in 
both symmetric and asymmetric relay positions whereas the BFGS and CG gradient 
methods converge only in symmetric cases. In the symmetric case, the BFGS method 
is the best choice as it is less complex compared with the other two, and in the asym-
metric case, the N-M method is the best method. Our results show that the optimiza-
tion algorithm and the achievable secrecy for different scenarios depend greatly on 
the position of the selected relay.
